**The knobs, for measuring rather than for flying**: `--present fifo|fifo-relaxed|mailbox|immediate|fifo-latest-ready` (by name, FIFO where the surface lacks one; `--no-vsync` is still mailbox, else immediate), `--swapchain-images <2..8>` (default 3) and `--frames-in-flight <1..3>` (default 2) for the window's loop, `--borderless` (a window with no decorations at the primary display's corner, [window](window.md#borderless-windows-and-presentation)), **`--fullscreen`** (the window starts in desktop fullscreen on its display, without a mode change, `Window::set_fullscreen`; refused offscreen with `--borderless`'s sentence), and **`--windowed`**, which keeps a `--replay-input` with `--benchmark` in the window at the recording's pace instead of offscreen: a replay of the owner's session in the window is the fair before and after of any change here. **With a `--camera-path` instead** (2026-10-03), it flies the path in the window **at the path's own speed in real time** — each frame draws the path frame its clock has reached since the first frame began, so a slow frame skips path frames rather than slowing the camera, and the run ends on the path's last frame — and `--benchmark` writes a record per presented frame with the window's waits, as a replay's; `--wait-quiet` waits for a quiet machine before the first frame. It is how the world's tiles are measured keeping up with a player's clock, where an offscreen flight waits for every frame's rebuild ([renderer](renderer.md#what-a-frame-waits-for); [the measurement](../experiments/world-tiles-window-2026-10-03.md)). Its records' `terrain` object carries the ground's layout as of version 3 (`layout_lag_m`, `layout_lag_frames`, `chunks_built`, `chunks_dropped`, `rebuild_ms`, `chunk_upload_bytes`, `host_ms`), and the summary's `time_lapse.rings` the most the layout fell behind (`max_lag_m`, `max_lag_frames`). None of them fixes the pairs without the pacer: at the spanned size mailbox and FIFO-latest-ready pair just the same (the driver paces them like FIFO when the window covers the display), one frame in flight misses refreshes (36 ms frames), and two images behave as three.

**Alt+Enter** (roadmap P46, 2026-10-18) switches any engine-view window between desktop fullscreen and windowed, as a game offers: `Window::set_fullscreen(!fullscreen())` ([window](window.md#borderless-windows-and-presentation)) — the display mode never changes, the platform answers with `Resized` and the loop recreates the swapchain from it as for any resize, and on the way back SDL restores the window's size and place; the title, the HUD and the result panel lay out from the frame's size and the display's scale as they already did. **The chord is the window's, not the game's.** The window's events carry no modifier bits, so the loop follows Alt from its own `KeyDown`/`KeyUp` (and forgets it on `FocusLost`, whose release goes to whoever took the focus); a non-repeat `KeyDown` of Enter with Alt held toggles, and that Enter is consumed before everything else in the loop reads the event — its press, its repeats, and the `KeyUp` that ends it, whenever Alt is let go, are dropped while a flag says the chord's Enter is down — so neither the result panel's Enter nor the edge's conversion to the session's `RawEvent`s ([input](input.md)) ever sees it: a game that binds Enter gets no press from the chord, and a `--record-input` log never contains it. Alt's own events pass on as before. So **a replay never performs it** (the recording has no chord in it, and an offscreen replay has no window), and an `--inject-input` run takes no chord from its log, because its window's size is the run's. A window's `--benchmark` summary counts the switches in `presentation.fullscreen_toggles` beside `borderless` and `fullscreen` (`--fullscreen`; `FlythroughPresentation` version 3), so a distribution that spans a toggle says so. Nobody's desktop takes a fullscreen window in a test: the toggle itself is verified by hand, and the end-to-end tests check `--fullscreen`'s refusal offscreen.

**A ceiling on a window its display does not pace** (`view.present.max_hz`, 120 by default; `--present-max-hz <n>` for one run, `0` for none). Mailbox, immediate and `--pace off` leave a frame's cadence to the swapchain's own waits, and a small window has none: a frame of 256×160 draws in microseconds and the window presents thousands of times a second. **Every present of a windowed swapchain goes through the desktop's compositor, and a flood of them stops the compositor composing**: on 2026-09-28, beside one run of this app's own tests, nothing on the owner's desktop repainted — every application, Task Manager included, with only the mouse cursor still moving, which is drawn on the display's own plane — for as long as such a window lived *and for most of a minute after it had closed* (seven stalls in a row, eighty-six seconds in all, measured by timing `DwmFlush()`; the owner could not type and reported it from his phone). It was not the GPU's load (3 to 38% busy), its memory (6.4 GB of 32 GB) or the processor. So the loop starts no frame sooner than a period after the one before it, by yielding until the time comes (the period is a few milliseconds and the system's sleep is coarser).

| Ceiling, presents a second | Stalls over 250 ms | Longest | Stalled in all |
|---|---|---|---|
| none (the tests as they were) | 7 over 700 ms | 20 s and more | 86 s |
| 480 | 13 | 6.1 s | 22.4 s |
| 360 | 10 | 2.5 s | 11.7 s |
| 300 | 20 | 0.4 s | 6.4 s |
| 240 | 0 | — | — |
| 120 | 0 | — | — |
| 60 | 0 | — | — |

(RTX 5090, the owner's desktop of 11520×2160 at 82 Hz, a 256×160 window drawing for twelve seconds per row, `msvc-debug`, under the GPU lock, the machine otherwise compiling; the first two rows are the `engine_view` test itself.) The stalls grow as a window lives — a quarter second, a half, one, three — which is a backlog building, and the line falls between 240 and 300, about three refreshes' worth of presents a refresh. **The default is 120, half the highest rate measured clean**, because the line was found on one display and will sit lower on a slower one. The ceiling applies only where the display pacer does not pace; a session is its ticks', so a recording replays to the same trajectory under any ceiling, and the test flies the fixture under two of them. The summary's `presentation` block says which ceiling the run had (`max_hz`, 0 where the display paced it) and how many frames it held back (`ceiling_waits`). A flight is vsync'd and paced by its display unless it asks otherwise, and is then untouched by the ceiling; one that asks for `--no-vsync` or `--pace off` is held to it at any size (at 11520×2160 a frame takes about two milliseconds, so such a flight would otherwise present some hundreds of times a second). A measurement that wants the loop's own rate names a higher ceiling or `0`, which warns on stderr, and is run **with nobody working at the desktop**.

**The frame log.** A window's `--benchmark` record carries the frame's waits beside `cpu_ms`: `wait_ms` (the frame slot), `acquire_ms`, `present_ms`, `pace_ms` and `pace_depth`, and `submit_ms`, the part of `cpu_ms` in the renderer's `submit_frame`; and, where the swapchain can say (VK_EXT_present_timing), `shown_ms`, from the previous present reaching the display to this one's, and `latency_ms`, from the frame sampling its input to its reaching the display; and, in an interactive session, the camera the frame drew (`pose_position` — a `worldpos`, three JSON doubles, since `FrameRecord` version 9 of 2026-10-05, where it was a float32 that 420 km out stood on a 3.1 cm grid — `pose_yaw`, `pose_pitch`) and the simulated time it stands for (`pose_time`), which is what says whether what the viewer saw advanced by a refresh a frame. The summary's `presentation` block says what the swapchain was (mode asked for and got, images asked for and got, frames in flight, the pacer, its misses and changes of depth, borderless, the refresh the driver reports, recreations and suboptimal presents) and gives the percentiles of every wait and display time. Two cautions, both measured: **asking for display times changes how FIFO paces on this driver** — a FIFO chain created with VK_EXT_present_timing never paired in five runs, and every one created without it paired, six runs out of six (present ids and waits alone change nothing) — so a measurement of the unpaced loop as the owner flies it is taken with **`--no-present-timing`**, which asks for none; and **the display times read early**: a frame with 8.3 ms of work between sampling and presenting cannot reach the display 3.6 ms after sampling, which is what they said, so `latency_ms` is low by an offset of at least 5 ms and only its differences between runs are to be trusted. The times are read once, at the end of the run, because on this driver reading them blocks for a refresh ([gfx](gfx.md), "Which present, how deep, and when it was shown").

**Testing.** `tests/fly_tests.cpp` drives `view::DisplayPacer` with made-up waits (steady frames stay at depth 1; a stall is one miss and not two; a compositor's late-then-early waits miss nothing; frames that miss every refresh go to depth 2 on the third, come back after the hold, and a try that fails at once doubles it; a timed-out wait counts for nothing, and two in a row stop the waiting for 120 frames; the refresh estimated from the waits' median when the swapchain gives none), checks `--present`'s names and the display-time bookkeeping by present id, and `tests/interactive_tests.cpp` checks the refusals (unknown modes and depths, the window's flags offscreen, `--windowed` without a replay) and, with a display and a device, that the fixture replayed with `--windowed --benchmark --pace display` flies the committed trajectory, has a record of waits for every presented frame with the ticks adding up to 480, and a `presentation` block naming FIFO, the images and the depth.

### Animation in a session

A scene's characters — instances with an `"animation"` block, or every skinned instance under `--animate` (which ones: "Mixed scenes", under engine-view's flags above) — animate **on the simulation clock**, and since roadmap S87 that clock is the same in every path that draws them (`apps/engine_view/animation.h`, "The clock"):

- **In a session** (`--interactive`, `--inject-input`, `--replay-input` in a window or offscreen, and a `--game`'s) the clock is the session's tick. `view::AnimationTicks` is the session's tick observer: it chains the one already set — the game runner's, which runs first, so a game that changes a clip at a tick changes it before that tick's step — and then brings the world up to the tick, step *k* due at tick `k * tick_hz / 60`, each step's tiers decided from **that tick's camera** (`FlySession::camera()`), never a frame's interpolated one. A live window, a window fed a recording and an offscreen replay of it therefore step the same world at the same ticks whatever their frame rates, and end on the same poses. A frame only takes the matrices as the ticks before it left them.
- **Without a session** the clock is the frame index, one step a frame, frame *f* drawn after step *f* + 1 — what `--frames N` always meant, in the window and now offscreen too, where before S87 a plain offscreen run, a replay and their captures ran no clips at all and drew every block's instance rigid. A frame drawn again from the same clock — a marker, the final `--capture` — steps nothing more; the world never steps back.
- **A timed flight** (`--offscreen --camera-path … --benchmark`) draws its characters in their rest pose and says so on stderr: `renderer::fly_camera_path` builds its own frames and hands them no joint matrices, so the census and the markers after it leave them at rest too and still compare like with like. Its block says `"clock":"none"`. Benchmark an animated scene in a window, or replay a recording offscreen.

**A game drives the characters it names** (ABI 5, roadmap S88): a `--game` module finds a named animated instance as an actor and moves it, changes its clip, its rate or stops it at a tick, through the same observer chain — its commands land before the tick's step ([Actors](#actors-the-characters-a-game-moves)).

**Frame-rate independence is the tick's, not the frame's.** A window on a slow frame runs several ticks and so several steps before it draws; on a fast one none, and it draws the pose it drew. Nothing about a step depends on when it ran — `SimWorld::step` reads no clock, and the tiers read a tick's camera — so the poses are a function of the tick count alone, and a recording replays to them bit for bit. The views a tier is decided from are the renderer's, so a window and a replay agree on a tier change only at the same extent: a test that pins hashes across the two either draws both at one size or keeps its characters in one band (`--anim-lod-scale`).

**The hashes.** The summary's animation block (`engine.scene.FlythroughAnimation`: the offscreen summary's and a window `--benchmark`'s `animation`, a window's summary line's `anim`, and through the summary line every `--report`) carries, besides the LOD numbers above, the clock — `clock` (`"ticks"`, `"frames"`, `"none"`), `clock_hz`, `clock_ticks`, and `steps`, always `clock_ticks * 60 / clock_hz` — and per animated instance (`animated`, `engine.scene.AnimatedInstance`) its scene index, the clip it was attached with, its tier at the end, and three hex u64s: `clip_hash` over the player's clip ids, `playhead_hash` over its time, rate and fade as bits, and `pose_hash` over the bytes of the joint matrices the last frame handed the renderer for it (an empty run — LOD2, LOD3, the rest pose — hashes as nothing). `hash` is over all of them in instance order. A game's test pins them: the same recording, offscreen or in a window, writes the same ones (`mesh_view_tests.cpp`, "an offscreen replay animates exactly as the window it was recorded in": the 480-tick fixture injected into a window and its recording replayed offscreen end on one `hash`, 120 steps each).

**Testing.** `mesh_view_tests.cpp`: a mixed scene (a rigid cube first, the skinned bar second, a `.flora.json` palm and a water block where the build has them) loads and draws offscreen with the bar posed — 30 steps, the second instance animated, the capture different from the same scene with no block, five frames a different pose and playhead; a block on the cube and on the palm, and a clip the bar does not have, are refused with sentences naming the instance; and the window/replay case above. `cluster_lod_tests.cpp` holds the merge.

## `--game`: running a game module

**engine-view runs a game** (`game_runner.h`, `game_runner.cpp`; roadmap P41). `--game <module.dll>` loads a hot-reloadable game module — a shared library built by `engine_game_module()` that links no engine library ([hotload](hotload.md)) — through `hotload::ModuleHost` and plays it from the walking session: `engine-view --scene <scene.json> --walk --game build/<preset>/bin/game/walk_poc.dll`. It implies `--interactive`, so it is a live session in a window, or a replay (`--replay-input <log> --game <module>`, in the window or `--offscreen`). The game itself is written elsewhere — in its own repository under [ADR-0047](../adr/0047-reference-games-are-licensed-apart-from-the-engine.md), against the contract below — and `game/walk_poc` is the fixture that proves the runner ([game/README.md](../../game/README.md)).

- **Loaded before any window or device**, so a module the host refuses is refused at once, exit 1, in the host's own words: no entry point, another ABI, a state type with an owning field, a path or an action that does not resolve (`engine-view: game: the module host refused …/walk_poc.dll (LoadHookFailed): on_load; no game action 'dance' (a module may read interact use game_1 game_2 game_3 game_4)`), a module that exports no `game_tick`, or a `--game-state` file of another state type.
- **Ticked on every tick, after the walker's and before the frame** — a tick observer on `view::FlySession` (`set_tick_observer`), called at the end of each tick in a live session, a replay and an injected run alike, so the module sees exactly the ticks the trajectory is made of and never moves the camera.
- **Rebuilt while it runs.** Between frames the runner polls the module's file (`ModuleHost::poll_changes`, one stat) and a rebuild written over it is swapped in with its state carried by the descriptors (a field added defaulted, one removed dropped); a refused rebuild keeps the old build running and says why once on stderr. This is the loop a game's author lives in: build the module, and the running session picks it up. It polls once a frame, not once a tick, because a stat costs about 0.23 ms and a tick is 4 ms at 240 Hz ([hotload](hotload.md#performance-notes)); a frame is between ticks either way, which is the host's one-thread rule.
- **Ending.** A module ends the run with `end_run(result)`. **In a live window the session stays on a result panel** (2026-10-17, roadmap P44; before, the window closed on the frame `end_run` was called and a tester saw their score for no frames at all): from that frame on, the HUD keeps the run's last lines and meters at the top left, and a panel in the picture's centre says `The run is over`, then the result object's fields in its order — each a key and its value formatted, a whole number plainly, a fraction to one decimal, a string as it is — then `Enter to quit · R for a new run · Esc to quit`, for as long as the tester likes. The session keeps ticking (the camera is theirs to turn, the sky's clock runs on from the runner's), the module is **not** ticked again (it said the run is over), and one line goes to the console. **Enter** and **Escape** end the session as a closed window does, the result in the summary's `interactive.game.result` and the report's `game.result`, the report's reason `end_run`. **R** starts a new run: the module's state object is put back at its descriptor's defaults in place by the host (`hotload::ModuleHost::reset_state`, host side only, nothing reloaded and `on_load` not called again: [hotload](hotload.md#the-module-abi)), the HUD cleared, the walker left where it stands, `interactive.game.runs` counting it and the ended run's result kept in `interactive.game.results[]`, so a tester's three attempts come back as three results in one report. **`--on-end panel|quit`** chooses: the panel is the default for somebody at the window (the package's), `quit` makes a live session end at `end_run` as a replay does (a run script may want it), and an **injected** session defaults to `quit` — a replay, an injected session and any offscreen run end at `end_run` exactly as they did unless `--on-end panel` is given, which is how the tests drive the panel. A module that calls `fail` from `game_tick` (or hands `end_run` something that is not a JSON object) ends the session everywhere with the line in `game.error`, the report's reason `game_error`; the process exits 0, because the session itself ended cleanly and printed its summary — never a crash and never a dialog.
- **`--game-state <file>`** saves the module's state at exit — `{"format":"engine.game.state","version":1,"module","type","tick","state":{…}}`, the state written through its own descriptor — and restores it at start when the file exists (unknown fields ignored, missing ones at their defaults: the reload's rules), so a run can be left and resumed. Without it a session starts the module fresh. A file of another state type is refused naming both. **Version 2 (2026-10-18, roadmap P47) resumes the session too**, not only the module: before, a resumed run had its state and its props back and the walker at the entrance. The file gains a `session` block — `{"version":1,"position":[x,y,z],"yaw","pitch","walking","speed_scale","sprint_scale","clock":{"sun_time_s","sun_offset_s","day_s","sun_rate","ground_time_s","surface_time_s","time_rate"}}`: the camera's eye as f64 world metres (a walker is dropped from it to the ground, as at any start), the look, whether it walked, the walker's speed scales as `walker_set` left them, the sky's time and rate as the keys left them, the ground time the world's clock stands on, the moving ground's time the last frame drew, and the time-lapse's rate. **Session version 2** (2026-10-20, roadmap R88; version 1 still reads, its world starting at 0 at real time) adds to the clock `sky_time_s` (the sky's hour, absolute — what a restore keeps, whatever the new session's epoch and `--time-of-day`), `day_length_s` (the sun rate said as the day's length, for a reader: the rate is what is restored, to its bits), `world_time_s` (the world clock, so the waves, the smoke and the sway carry on from their phase) and `world_rate` (as the keys left it, which the dunes then ride). Restoring it: a **live** session starts there (`live_header`; `--start` still wins); the runner's clock takes the sky's time whatever this session's `--time-of-day` offset is, and the rate; the walker's scales go on before the first tick; and the scene's ground is read at the saved surface time with the time-lapse at its rate, unless `--ground-time` or `--time-rate` says otherwise. A version 1 file restores the state and props as before and no session; a block of another version is refused naming it. **Since ABI 5 (2026-10-19) the file also carries `actors`** — each named actor's placement and player, restored before the first tick ([Actors](#actors-the-characters-a-game-moves)). The time-lapse's rate saved is the one the session started with (`--time-rate`), not one changed by keys in the window, which the runner does not see. **A replay is still a replay**: a live session that restored a state writes the object it restored into its recording's header (`game.restored`, fly_camera.h `SessionHeader::game_restored`), and a replay of a `--game` recording restores **from its header** — that object, or nothing for a recording that started fresh — and never from `--game-state`, which the live session overwrote at its end (a replay given one says so once, and writes it at its end). A recording without a game block (a synthetic one, or one from before 2026-10-17) restores from the file as before, keeping its own start. Tested with no GPU in `game_runner_tests.cpp` (the block written after a second of walking at a sun rate of 60, read back with the sky's time kept across another offset, the pose put on a header, a version 1 file without one, a version 9 block refused, a replay's restore from its header and not the file) and offscreen in `game_view_tests.cpp` (a recording whose header restored the first run's file replays to its state plus one second and the sky 60 game seconds on, to the same trajectory with or without a `--game-state` of other numbers, which it overwrites and does not read).
- **What it costs with no module**: nothing — no host, no observer, no poll; `--game` in a build without the hotload capability (`ENGINE_WITH_HOTLOAD=OFF`, the minimal build) exits 3 saying the build has no module host.

### The game contract

Everything a game shares with the engine is the hotload ABI's vocabulary ([module_abi.h](../../foundation/hotload/include/foundation/hotload/module_abi.h), ABI 6, which loads modules built against 3, 4 and 5 unchanged: [hotload](hotload.md#the-module-abi)): typed `Value`s read through views by field paths resolved by name, one schema struct of state, and the `HostApi` table. **Why nothing new**: a game is rebuilt under a running engine and lives in another repository, so what it shares has to survive both sides changing; resolving by name turns an engine-side rename into a refusal naming the path rather than a wrong read, and the state's descriptor carries it across a rebuild.

- **The export**: `game_tick`, a `TickFn` — `void game_tick(ModuleContext*, View tick, f32 dt)` — called every tick. It reads its `tick` view's fields (resolve them in `on_load` against `engine.game.GameTick`), changes its own state, and says what the HUD shows.
- **`engine.game.GameTick`** (`apps/engine_view/game_tick.schema`): `tick` (u64, from 1) and `dt` (f64 seconds — read this one rather than the f32 argument, whose 1/240 drifts a part in ten million a tick when summed); `eye` and `feet` (dvec3, world metres, f64; the feet are the eye while flying); `heading` and `pitch` (f32 radians, heading about +y counter-clockwise from above, 0 along −z); `walking`, `on_ground`, `sprinting` (bool); `moved_m` (f64, metres over the ground this tick, 0 flying); `sun_time_s` (the sky's seconds past the scene's epoch: `FrameDesc::sky_time_s`, the `--time-of-day` offset plus the day's game seconds at the session's day length, stepped by `[` and `]` at the ticks they are pressed on), `world_time_s` (**the world clock**, real seconds scaled by the world rate, stepped by `,` and `.`: what a game animates by — until 2026-10-20 it was the sky's hour, which a game that counted days by it now reads as `sky_time_s`) and `day_fraction` (where the **sky** is in its day, 0 at midnight to 1); appended in `GameTick` version 2 (R88): `sky_time_s` (the sky's hour, absolute: the epoch plus `sun_time_s`, what the walk_poc and the template count their days by), `day_length_s` (real seconds, 0 a still sky) and `world_rate` (0 pauses every animation); `actions_pressed`, `actions_held`, `actions_released` (u64 masks by the bits `game_action` handed out; `hotload::action_set(value, bit)` reads one).
- **The services** (`HostApi`, each `host` first, the module-side helpers in `module_abi.h`): `game_action(name, &bit)` in `on_load` — `interact`, `use`, `game_1`..`game_4` and nothing else (an unknown name refuses the load, listed with any field path that did not resolve; an action the session's map lacks, as in a log recorded before revision 5, is accepted and never pressed); `hud_text(line, text)` and `hud_meter(name, value, max)`, which the host clears at the start of every tick, so a module says each tick what the HUD shows (16 lines of 95 characters, 8 meters; more is dropped); `log(level, text)` (the existing one: a record in the `hotload` category); `end_run(json)`; and `nearest_placement(kind, radius, &position, &distance)`, the nearest placement of a scatter's or the ruins' kind within `radius` metres of the walker's feet, `NotFound` when there is none or the session streams no world. It is answered by the streamed world (`view::ViewWorld::nearest_placement`): **a kind is a streamed entry's generator name** (`ruins`, `scatter`), an instance is of that kind when its mesh is one of that entry's resident meshes, the distance is over the ground (x and z), and since 2026-10-17 (roadmap P44) **the answer is a function of the scene, not of what has streamed in**: every tile the search reaches is asked of the placements consumer's query cache (`PlacementTiles::query_tile`, [world](world.md#the-consumers)) — generated from the scene on the first ask, in the innermost ring's representation on the scene's own ground, and kept while the walker is within the cap of it — never of the ring's held blocks, so a recording, its replay and a capture answer alike whatever the ring holds (before, a radius past the ring saw only what had streamed in, and the fixture's "nearest ruin" line changed with the streaming); the radius is capped at **`k_max_placement_query_m` = 512 m**, past which the answer is `InvalidArgument` (a `Result` a module already handles; `placements.refused` counts them), because a cold query generates every tile out to the first square that can hold nothing nearer than what it found — `placements.generated` and `generate_ms` say how many and how long; `game_placements_tests.cpp`'s 400 m look at four ruins 226 m off generates 225 tiles cold — a 15-tile square, four of them with a building to assemble in blocks, the rest `occupies` answers — in 1.1 ms of generator time (Debug, the machine shared), and the 6 m look every tick after it hits the cache; a cold look that finds nothing generates the whole 33² of the cap. The test streams the same recording with one 48 m ring and with three rings to 320 m and holds the fixture's water line and its found count equal — `water: a ruin 226 m away` in both, where the one-ring world used to see no ruin at all. **Nothing else**: no document, no renderer, no files. A module changes its own state and what the HUD shows.
- **Acting on the world** (ABI 3, 2026-10-17, roadmap P43): the services through which a module changes something besides its state and the HUD, each in the ABI's style and each answered `Unsupported` by a host that runs no game. **Props** — `prop_mesh(name, &mesh)` in `on_load` (the index of a prop the scene declared, `NotFound` remembered and the load refused naming it, `Unsupported` — the scene declares none — a plain answer, so one module runs on any scene), `prop_place(mesh, position[3], yaw, scale, &prop)`, `prop_move(prop, position[3], yaw)`, `prop_remove(prop)` and `nearest_prop(mesh | k_any_prop, radius, &prop, &position[3], &distance)`, over the ground from the walker's feet like `nearest_placement` — are the section below. **The walker** — `walker_set(speed_scale, sprint_scale)`, each held to [0, 4], 1 the recording's `WalkParams` to the bit (`x * 1.0f` is `x`, and the walker multiplies only there: every walk recorded before the scales existed replays as it did), applied from the next tick; and `walker_teleport(position[3], heading, pitch)`, the walker's feet put at the position on this tick, standing on the ground under it, the camera with it (flying, the camera itself goes there) looking along `heading` at `pitch` (held to the session's limit), `InvalidArgument` for a non-finite number or a position outside the world. A module reads the effect through `feet` and `moved_m` next tick; `GameTick` gains nothing. The requests are made by the tick observer and applied by the session **inside the same tick, before it is hashed** (`FlySession::request_teleport`, `set_walk_scale`), so **a recorded `--game` session replays to the same trajectory with the same module** — and the session header gains a `game` block (`module`, `build`), on which **a replay of a `--game` recording without `--game`, or with a module of another name, is refused** before anything is loaded: `engine-view: replay: <log> was recorded with the game module 'walk_poc_teleport' (build 1), whose calls can move the walker; replay it with --game and that module, or it would walk somewhere else without saying so`, and `… was recorded with the game module 'walk_poc_teleport' and --game names 'walk_poc'; another module would walk the recording somewhere else, so it is refused`. A header without the block replays as it always did. **Sound** — `sound_clip`, `sound_play`, `sound_stop` — is in the table so that the audio half does not change the ABI again, and **is not built**: engine-view answers `Unsupported` and says so once (`the module asks for sound, which engine-view does not play yet`). The summary's `game` block gains `walker` (`sets`, `teleports`, `speed_scale`, `sprint_scale`, `session`), and the walk's block `teleports`, `speed_scale` and `sprint_scale`.
- **The action map, as a file** (2026-10-18, roadmap P47): `engine-view --dump-action-map <file.json>` writes the map a session would fly with — `--input-map`'s, or engine-view's own at its latest revision (`k_fly_map_revision`) — as `ActionMap::to_json` writes it, plus `hash` (the number a recording's header carries), `hash_hex`, `revision` (0 for a map of the caller's) and `source`, and exits with one JSON line; no window, no device. The file loads back through `--input-map` (the reader ignores the added keys). It is how a game pins the map its sessions are recorded against: by the file, not by a hash typed into a test ([game-authoring](../game-authoring.md)).
- **The HUD's elements** (ABI 4, 2026-10-18, roadmap P47): `hud_element(element)` places or replaces a `HudElement` by its id and the host keeps it until `hud_remove(id)`, a new run, or the module says it again; [The HUD, provisional](#the-hud-provisional) has the model. `InvalidArgument` (said once on stderr, counted in `hud.element_refused`) for a reserved id, an enum out of range, a length that is not finite or a size at or below zero, a NUL inside the text's length; `OutOfRange` past 64 elements. The helpers answer `Unsupported` on a host whose table ends before them (`ENGINE_HOTLOAD_HAS`). The summary's `game` block gains `abi` (what the module declared) and `hud_elements`, and `hud` gains `elements`, `element_sets`, `element_removes`, `element_refused`.
- **The graphics, read and asked for** (ABI 4, 2026-10-18, roadmap P47): `graphics_state(&state)` fills a `GraphicsState` with what the session **resolved** — `raster` and `shadows` by the renderer's names (`renderer::raster_name`, `resolved_shadow_name`; the report's `machine` block says the same), `present` (the mode asked for, or `offscreen`; the swapchain's own is the summary's `presentation`), `display` (the encoding the frames are drawn for: `sdr`, `hdr10`, `scrgb`, `linear`, filled from the first frame), `tier`, `width`, `height`, `ray_tracing` (the frame builds acceleration structures), `hdr` — and up to four `reasons` it is not what was asked: a vertex path on a device with no mesh shaders, cascaded maps on one with no ray queries, back faces casting no shadow under traced shadows. **The reasons are derived in engine-view** from the device's features and the resolved flags (`note_game_graphics`, main.cpp), because `renderer::resolve_settings` logs its overrides and keeps no record of them; a reason the renderer adds to its rules is not here until someone adds it beside them, and the renderer keeping its own list (`ResolvedSettings::overrides`) is the fix, which this change does not make. `Unsupported` before there is a device (`on_load`). `request_graphics(request)` **records** a change — names that are empty leave a setting as it is — and answers `Unsupported` for one engine-view cannot make at all (a raster path, shadow mode, present mode or display it has not, ray tracing on a device without ray queries, HDR on a session drawn for SDR), `InvalidArgument` for a name with no NUL inside its array or a size past 16,384. **Nothing applies a request yet**: the next safe point is a restart of the session, which engine-view does not do; the summary's `game.graphics` block (`known`, the state, `requests`, `refused_requests`, `last_refused`, and the last recorded request as `pending`) is how a launcher or a tester sees what the game asked for. No swapchain is rebuilt under a module. The fixture reads the state on its first tick and logs it, and makes one request of each kind (`shadows: csm`, recorded; `raster: warp`, refused), which the prompt test above asserts beside the report's machine block.
- **Actors and the ground** (ABI 5, 2026-10-19, roadmap S88): `actor_find`, `actor_place`, `actor_play`, `actor_stop`, `actor_speed`, `actor_state` — the scene's named skinned instances, moved and posed at the tick — `actor_spawn`, answered `Unsupported` until the renderer's tail draws a deformed instance, and `ground_height(x, z)`, the walker's own ground; [Actors](#actors-the-characters-a-game-moves) below has the model, the state file's `actors` and what a replay re-runs.
- **The HUD's images and the game's fonts** (ABI 6, 2026-10-19, roadmap P49): `hud_image` — a PNG or JPEG from the game's content directory (beside the scene file, then beside the module) as a retained, nine-sliced, tinted HUD element — and `hud_font` — a TrueType or OpenType file the game ships, a handle a `HudElement` names in its new `font` field beside `letter_spacing_px` and a drop shadow; an ABI 4 or 5 element is read only as far as its `size` and draws as it did ([The HUD, provisional](#the-hud-provisional)).
- **Determinism**: every `GameTick` field is a function of the session's ticks (the clock is advanced a tick at a time in the runner, not read from a frame — and since 2026-10-17 **the frame is drawn from it**: a `--game` session's `FrameDesc::sky_time_s` and `world_time_s` are the runner's `sun_time_s()` and `world_time_s()` in the window, in an offscreen replay and in a `--capture`, copied into the host's `SunDay` and `WorldDay` by `game_clock` so the title's rates and the summary's `sun` block say the same, which is how a replay of a recording in which `]` was pressed draws the sky where the live session drew it, where before an offscreen replay lit frame f by its index at the flag's rate; `game_end_tests.cpp` replays one second with and without the press at `--sun-rate 3600` and holds the renderer's `sun.sky.time_s` to the runner's `clock.sky_time_s` in both, and the world clock to the same second in both, 4,641 game seconds and 12,826 of 15,360 pixels apart), so **a recorded `--game` session replays to the same module state**, offscreen or in the window, as the walk replays to the same trajectory; a module's code adds nothing machine-dependent as long as it reads no wall clock (it has none through the ABI) and computes with the engine's flags (it is compiled with them). `on_tick` allocates nothing on either side: the view, the HUD's lines and meters and the action table are fixed arrays in the runner, the module's state is the one object the host allocated when it loaded the module.

### Props: the objects a game places

**A game acts on the world through props** (`game_props.h`, `game_props.cpp`; roadmap P43, 2026-10-17): instances of meshes the scene file declared as `props` (`engine.scene.Prop`: `{"name": "can", "mesh": "can.glb"}`, with the same `hash` and `fit` a mesh takes; [renderer](renderer.md#scenes-camera-paths-and-flythroughs), "Props"), loaded with the scene and placed by nothing but the module. **Why a scene-file block**: the renderer's rule for instances that come and go is that the meshes stay and only the instances change ([renderer](renderer.md#instances-that-come-and-go)), so a module's objects have to be meshes the scene loaded, and the scene is where a game's author says which. The host owns the table (`view::GameProps`: f64 positions, a yaw and a uniform scale per prop, `k_max_props` = 1,024 — a constant and not a tunable, because a tick allocates nothing on either side of the boundary and a prop id packs its slot in its low bits, so the cap is the id's shape), and the live props reach the picture as **one `renderer::DynamicBlock` keyed `view::k_game_block_key`** in the renderer's tail: when the scene streams a world, appended after the tiles' blocks by `ViewWorld::hand_over` (`set_extra_block`, and `hand_over_now` for a frame on which a prop changed and no tile did; [world](world.md#the-hosts)), and when it streams nothing, the one block that is the whole tail — the scene made dynamic by `--game` **only when it lists props** (`attach_game_scene`, main.cpp), so a scene with none stays exactly the scene it was, the frame loop's no-allocation rule and the budgets flown over it untouched. The block is handed over on the frames a prop changed and on no other (`GameProps::changed`/`handed`; the summary's `props.rewrites` counts them), and a frame with no change costs the props nothing: the renderer compares a block that comes back under its key and writes nothing for one that is the same. A prop id is `slot | generation << 10`, so a removed prop's id stays dead for the run rather than naming whatever took its slot. **A prop is visual**: the walker walks through it; the ray tracing chain and the shadows see it as they see the tail's instances. Collision with props is roadmap P44. **A prop may carry a fire** (`engine.scene.Prop.fire`, version 2; roadmap R85): every placed instance of it is a flickering light in the frame and, where the scene has a medium, a flame and its smoke, standing at the prop's origin plus the fire's offset turned and scaled with it — the renderer finds them in the tail it is handed ([renderer](renderer.md#fires)), so a game lights a campfire with `prop_place` and the ABI gains nothing (`game_props_view_tests.cpp` places one from the state file). **The state file carries them**: `--game-state` writes a `props` array beside `state` (`[{"id","prop","position":[x,y,z],"yaw","scale"}]`, the name rather than a mesh index, the position as JSON doubles) and restores it before the first tick — each prop back in its slot at its id, so a module's state that holds a prop's id means what it meant — and a resumed run has its objects without the module placing them again; a replay places them again, because the module's calls are a function of the ticks. A file naming a prop the scene does not declare is refused before any device, by name. The summary's `game` block gains `props` (`declared`, `placed`, `moved`, `removed`, `live`, `rewrites`, `queries`, `found`).

**The fixture does it** ([game/walk_poc](../../game/walk_poc/README.md)): on a scene declaring `can` and `marker` it places the can at the spring on the first tick, picks it up with `interact` within 2 m and fills the meter, places a marker where the walker stands on `use`, and below half thirst slows the walker (to half speed at zero); on a scene with no props it places nothing. **Testing** (`tests/game_props_tests.cpp`, no GPU; `tests/game_props_view_tests.cpp`, offscreen over a scene of the test's own — the renderer's waves and a box declared twice): the table's services, refusals and cap, ids across removal and reuse, the state file's bits, the reader's block; the fixture placing the can, picking it up and marking, a scene with no props running it with nothing placed and a scene with other props refusing it by name; end to end, a can restored from the state file changes 8,613 pixels in one blob at 800×450 and nothing else, a scene made dynamic with nothing placed draws 0 pixels differently outside the HUD's corner from the run without the game, the fixture's can at the spring is drawn with one hand-over and a second run from the state file has it without placing it, and **a live walk in a window on the test's desktop with the teleporting build replays offscreen to the same trajectory hash with the same module, and is refused without `--game` and with the plain fixture**. What a frame with no prop change costs the props: nothing — no hand-over is made; what one with a change costs is the renderer's compare over the tail and the block's slots written ([renderer](renderer.md#instances-that-come-and-go)).

### Actors: the characters a game moves

**A game moves the scene's characters through actors** (hotload ABI 5, 2026-10-19, roadmap S88; `animation.h` `GameActors`, `game_props.h` `DeclaredActor`/`ActorHost`, the runner's services in `game_runner.cpp`). Where a prop is a mesh the module places from nothing, an **actor** is a skinned instance the scene file already holds and **names**: an instance with a `"name"` that animates — an `animation` block, or any instance under `--animate` (`{"mesh":0,"name":"surveyor","translation":[…],"ground":true,"animation":{"clip":"idle"}}`; `name` was already in `engine.scene.Instance`, so the schema did not change). **Its handle is its scene instance's index** — the file's instances come first in the scene, so the index is stable for the file — found by name with `actor_find(name, &actor)`, which a module may call in `on_load` (a name the scene does not have refuses the load, listed with the names it has: `no actor 'bar' in the scene (it names tortoise)`; a scene with no actors answers `Unsupported`, so one module runs on any scene) or at any tick (`NotFound` is then a plain answer). **An unnamed instance is not reachable**: an index into the file would make a module depend on the order of a file it does not own, where a name survives the file being edited; a duplicate name is the first instance's, said once on stderr. The names are read with the scene file before the module loads (`read_scene_actors`, the props' pattern), and the table is bound once the animated world has its entities (`attach_game_actors`, main.cpp), the same place in the window's loop and the offscreen one; a named instance that turns out rigid (a mesh with no skin under `--animate`) is left out with a sentence, and its handle answers `NotFound`.

- **The services** (module_abi.h; each `host` first, the module-side helpers answering `Unsupported` on an ABI 4 table): `actor_place(actor, position[3], yaw)` stands the instance's origin at a world position (three f64), turned `yaw` radians about +y, **level** — the scene's pitch and roll are dropped and its scale kept, so a character stands up — `InvalidArgument` for a non-finite number or a position off the world; `actor_play(actor, clip, fade_s, looping)`, a clip of the actor's skeleton by its library name (`bar/bend`), its name without the file's prefix (`bend`) or its index as digits, cross-faded over `fade_s` by the capability's own fade (from the rest pose, a fade in of the player's weight), `NotFound` for a clip the skeleton has not; `actor_stop(actor, fade_s)`, a fade to the rest pose (0: at once); `actor_speed(actor, rate)`, the playback rate (1 the clip's own, 0 holds, negative backwards; absolute, not a multiple of the block's `speed`); `actor_state(actor, &state)`, an `ActorState` — position, yaw, the clip it is playing or fading into, the playhead, rate, weight, looping, playing, fading, tier, and **the pose hash of the last step**, the summary's `pose_hash` as a number; and **`ground_height(x, z, &height)`**, the scene's ground provider at the nearest millimetre — the walker's own `ground_at` (`Walker::ground_height`), so an actor stood on it stands where the walker would, deterministic, `Unsupported` for a scene with no ground. The ground query is the one the game's request 5 asked for, and it is not an actor's: a module may ask it for anything it places.
- **How a command reaches the tick.** The module runs in the game's tick observer, which the animated world's (`view::AnimationTicks`) calls first, so **a play, a stop or a rate is applied to the world at the tick the module made it, before that tick's animation step** — the same tick in a live window, an injected one and an offscreen replay. The stop's fade and a fade in are the player's `weight` (`AnimationPlayer::weight`, which the sampler already blends toward the bind pose), advanced a whole tick at a time by a hook between the game and the step (`AnimationTicks::before`) and written as the component's own bytes (`ecs::WorldCommands::set_bytes`), because the capability's surface has a cross-fade between clips and no stop; the fade ends on a tick count, never on a sum of 1/240s. A move changes the actor's placement and its tier row (`AnimatedScene::positions`, so the LOD decides from where it stands) at the tick, and **every actor moved since the last frame reaches the renderer in one `SceneRenderer::move_instances` before the next frame** (`GameActors::flush`, beside the props' hand-over): one write a frame and not one a tick, because in a scene read whole the call waits for the device, and four waits a frame at 240 Hz would stall the frames in flight for nothing a frame can show (a dynamic scene's move waits for nothing; below). So a move reaches the picture on the frame after its tick, as a prop does, while the pose of that tick is already drawn. The path is sampled a quarter-second at a time after the step (`AnimationTicks::after`), 256 samples an actor, sized at the bind; nothing on a tick allocates but a command's own small write.
- **A scene with props or a world moves its actors too** (2026-10-19). `--game` makes a scene that declares props dynamic, as a streamed world is, and until then `move_instances` refused a scene whose instances come and go, so there `actor_place` answered `Unsupported`. Now a dynamic scene's move of a loaded instance — an actor is one, the scene file's — **rides the next frame's table flip** as a prop's arrival does ([renderer](renderer.md#instances-that-come-and-go)): written into the CPU's table, marked for every table set, copied into the set the next frame flips to, and waiting for nothing, so a scene with props pays no wait for its actors at all. `actor_calls.can_move` is true in every scene, and the stderr line is gone. What remains of S88 is spawning (below).
- **What is state and what is replayed.** The module's commands are not recorded: a replay re-runs the module from what the recording restored, and its calls recur at the same ticks, so its actors walk the same path to the same poses. What `--game-state` keeps is the **result**: the state file gains `actors` — `[{"name","instance","position":[x,y,z],"yaw","player":{…},"fade":{"from","to","ticks","elapsed_ticks","stop"}}]`, the player as `engine.animation.AnimationPlayer`'s schema writes it (clip ids, times, rate, weight, looping) and a weight fade in flight — and **a cold restore puts every actor back before the first tick**: where it stood, in the clip at the playhead and rate it had. The names are checked when the state loads (a file naming an actor the scene does not is refused before any device, as a prop is), and a replay of a `--game` recording restores them from its header's `game.restored`, as it does the props and the session.
- **Spawning is refused for now.** `actor_spawn(mesh, position, yaw, &actor)` is in the table so that landing it does not change the ABI again, and answers `Unsupported` (said once): a run-time actor is a skinned instance in the renderer's dynamic tail, which the renderer refuses by name (roadmap R84, "a deformed tail instance is refused by name and is the next stage"). It needs that stage — the tail's instances given pool blocks and deform descriptors as the loaded prefix's are — then a pose-pool slot and an entity made at the tick (`AnimationSystem::attach` on a new `Id128`), the tier row appended, and the actor written to the state file with its mesh so a cold restore makes it again.
- **An actor does not collide.** The walker walks through it, as through a prop (the game checks reach itself, from `actor_state`'s position). A capsule per actor would need a kinematic body in `scene_collision`'s world moved to the actor's placement at the tick (before the walker's step, so the walker meets where the actor stands that tick), and the walker's ground-follow build — which has no bodies at all — to stay walking through it, saying so.

The summary's `game` block (and so the report's) gains **`actors`** — per actor `name`, `instance`, `position`, `yaw`, `clip`, `playhead`, `speed`, `weight`, `looping`, `playing`, `fading`, `tier`, `pose_hash`, and `path` (the quarter-second samples: `tick`, `position`, `yaw`, `clip`, `playhead`, `pose_hash`) with `path_dropped` — and **`actor_calls`** (`bound`, `actors`, `can_move`, `places`, `plays`, `stops`, `speeds`, `refused`, `flushes`, `moves`, `ground_queries`), and `ground` (whether the session has one). Before a world is bound (a build without animation), `actors` lists the declared names and `actor_calls.bound` is false.

**Testing.** With no GPU (`tests/game_runner_tests.cpp`, the actor build of the fixture against a hand-made `ActorHost`): a scene without `bar` refuses the load naming what it has, a scene with no actors runs with nothing walked, a found actor's commands answer `Unsupported` until a world is bound, and bound, 300 ticks walk it round a 3 m circle on a sloped ground it asked for, facing along its way, `bend` played once, the rate doubled at tick 120 and a stop over half a second at 240; a state file naming another actor is refused by name. End to end (`tests/game_actors_view_tests.cpp`, offscreen, the waves ground and mesh_view_tests' skinned bar named `bar`): a 480-tick replay with `walk_poc_actor` places the bar 480 times in 119 renderer writes, its eight path samples each 3 m from where it stood with the ground's height, `bar/bend` with a new pose hash at ticks 60–240 and one rest-pose hash (`91cf5420187ef200`) from 360; a second replay writes the same actor block and the same animation hash; its capture differs from the plain fixture's, which leaves the bar where the scene stood it, in 1,044 of 64,000 pixels; and a cold restore from a 200-tick run's state file stands the bar at the saved position to the bit, in `bar/bend` at rate 2, with no place made. The same replay on the scene with the fixture's two props declared — dynamic, the can placed on the first tick — walks the bar too: `can_move` true, 480 places in 119 renderer writes with none refused and no `Unsupported` line, the eight samples on the circle and no two alike, a second replay the same actor block and animation hash, and its capture 1,044 pixels from the plain fixture's (which places the same can and leaves the bar standing), the count the scene without props gives (2026-10-19).

### The HUD, provisional

**This is a proof-of-concept HUD, not the game UI framework**, which plan [04 §4.7](../plan/04-renderer.md) and 10 §10.6 R45 defer; [ADR-0054](../adr/0054-the-tools-ui-is-dear-imgui-drawn-by-the-renderer.md) says a tool's overlay is not a HUD, and its status note of 2026-10-16 says this one borrows the tools' path without becoming that decision. What a module said on its last tick — `hud_text` lines and `hud_meter` meters — is laid out by Dear ImGui as **one fixed panel at the top left of the picture's safe region** (5% in from each edge, at least 8 px): the meters first, each a name and a horizontal bar with `value / max` on it, then the lines in order. No input reaches it and nothing on it is interactive (`ImGuiWindowFlags_NoInputs`, no decoration, no saved settings). `view::GameHudOverlay` (`game_hud.h`) builds the frame's draw lists and hands them to the renderer as `FrameDesc::overlay`, the overlay pass composites them after everything else the frame drew ([renderer](renderer.md#the-overlay)) — so it is in a window's picture, in an offscreen frame and in a `--capture`, and the visibility buffer under it is still the scene's. **The pass is engine-editor's**: its textures (ImGui's font atlas becomes one overlay texture, a HUD image another) and its composite, which on an HDR target is at paper white (below). **What it costs**: with no module, nothing — no ImGui context is made and no frame names an overlay; with one, an ImGui frame of a few widgets per drawn frame and its lists copied into vectors that keep their capacity, and a HUD that says nothing draws nothing. **On an HDR target** (`--present-format hdr10|scrgb`; 2026-10-19, roadmap P49) the HUD is drawn too: the renderer composites the overlay in linear light at paper white before the output encode, so the HUD's white is `--paper-white-nits` (PQ's code for it, or scRGB's `paper_white / 80`), its transparency blends in linear light and nothing is encoded twice ([renderer](renderer.md#the-overlay)); before, the overlay pass refused it and the HUD was left off.

**It scales with the picture** (2026-10-17, roadmap P44; before, it was ImGui's 13 px bitmap font at every size — most of a 320×200 picture, unreadable on a 4K window). The font is rasterized at `hud_font_px(height, display_scale)`: **`k_hud_font_px` = 18 px on a 1080-pixel-high picture at 100 %**, other heights and display scales (`Window::display_scale`, 1.5 at 150 %, read at the start and on a resize or a `ScaleChanged`; 1 offscreen) in proportion, whole pixels, clamped to [10, 64]. Why 18: the panel is laid inside a television's action-safe region (5 % in from each edge), and broadcast practice puts the smallest readable caption at about 1/40 of the picture height — 27 px at 1080p — for a viewer three picture heights away; a desk is about half that distance, so 18 px (1/60) subtends from a desk what a caption does from the sofa, with margin, while sixteen lines at ImGui's leading take about 375 px, a third of the safe region's 972, and leave the picture to the picture; the floor is where ProggyClean stops being readable, the ceiling keeps a small window on a large display from one line across it. The meters' name column and bar (96 and 200 px at 13) and the style's paddings scale with the font (`ImGuiStyle::ScaleAllSizes`); the safe margin was already the picture's 5 %. **How, and what it costs.** ImGui 1.92's fonts are dynamic: `style.FontSizeBase` is the size glyphs are rasterized at, so a size is baked into the atlas when a frame first draws it — a resize, never a frame — and the atlas texture is re-uploaded then through the overlay's existing texture path (no renderer change). A layout after a size change carries the bake, about 0.85 ms in a Debug build against 0.06–0.13 ms for the same frame at the same size (`game_hud_tests.cpp` times both); a 30-frame capture uploads the atlas three times — the first frame's, then a glyph or two first used — for 1.7–3.6 ms of CPU in all (`interactive.game.overlay`: `font_px`, `panel`, `layouts`, `atlas_uploads`, `atlas_ms`), and a frame whose size and glyphs did not change uploads nothing and costs what it did.

**Structured elements** (ABI 4, 2026-10-18, roadmap P47). The stack above answers "what does the module say this tick"; a game also needs a prompt where the eye is, a counter in a corner, a caption that reads over a bright sky — and a game's test needs to know where they stood. `hud_element` gives a module **`hotload::HudElement`**: a stable `id`; one of **nine anchors** on the safe region (its corners, the centres of its edges, its centre) and an `offset_px` from it (x right, y down); an `align` across (the box's left edge, centre or right edge on the point; down, the anchor's row hangs the box from a top point, centres it on a middle one and stands it on a bottom one); UTF-8 `text` (127 bytes kept, cut at a character boundary); `color` (RGBA8); `size_px`; `outline_color` and `outline_px` (the text drawn eight times round its place, which costs the atlas nothing); a `panel` (none, or a solid colour with `panel_padding_px`); and `visible`. **The host owns scaling and accessibility**: every length is in pixels of a 1080-pixel-high picture at 100 % and is multiplied by the HUD's scale — the picture's height over 1080 times the display's scale, the factor the stack's `hud_font_px` uses — and the font holds the stack's floor of 10 px (`hud_element_px`, ceiling 128 px, since every size drawn is baked into the atlas once), so a module never sees the window and its 28 px prompt is 12 px at 450, 56 at 2160. **Retained, unlike the stack**: an element stands until it is removed, hidden, said again under its id, or a new run (R) clears the HUD, because a prompt or a counter changes on events and a module that had to repeat it every tick would be paying for the stack's model where it does not fit (saying it every tick is allowed and costs a copy). Elements are drawn on ImGui's background list in the order they were first placed, under the stack's panel and the result panel, at whole pixels. **`hud_text` and `hud_meter` are elements too**, of the host's reserved ids (`k_hud_line_id + n`, `k_hud_meter_id + m`, the top-left stack), and are laid out as the one panel they always were: a module that calls only them draws the picture it drew before elements existed, and the HUD captures below hold it to that. The first consumer is the fixture's `[E] Pick up` — outlined text, no panel, centred 80 px under the picture's centre while the can is in reach.

**Images, the game's own fonts and styled text** (ABI 6, 2026-10-19, roadmap P49). A game's UI is mostly graphics and stylised type, so a module can also place **an image** — `hud_image(const HudImage&)`: an element of the same id space and the same nine anchors, alignments and 1080p offsets, drawing a PNG or JPEG **from the game's content directory** (`path`, `/`-separated and relative: looked up beside the scene file, then beside the module; an absolute path or a `..` is `InvalidArgument`, a file in neither place `NotFound`, one `foundation/image` cannot decode `InvalidArgument`) at `size_px` (both 0: the image's own pixels as 1080p pixels; one 0: that side from the other by the image's aspect), multiplied by `tint`, and with **nine-slice borders** (`slice_px`, left, top, right, bottom in the image's pixels, drawn at the HUD's scale) so one small PNG is a panel of any size with its corners kept. The host decodes a path once, when it is first named (validating it), and uploads it as an overlay texture the first time a frame draws it — in the HUD's build before the frame is recorded, never in the frame's record — keeps it while any element names it and removes it with the last such element; sixteen paths at most (`OutOfRange` past them, and past the table's 64 elements as before). And **a font** — `hud_font(path, size_px, &font)`: a TrueType or OpenType file from the same directories (its signature checked: `InvalidArgument` for anything else), added to the HUD's ImGui atlas beside the default font and baked at each element's own size on the host's scale ladder as the default font is (`hud_element_px`: 1080p pixels times the HUD's scale, whole pixels, 10 to 128), eight at most; the same path twice is the same handle. `HudElement` grew at its end — read only as far as a module's `size` says, so an ABI 4 or 5 module's elements draw exactly as they drew — by **`font`** (0 the HUD's own; else a handle `hud_font` returned, `InvalidArgument` otherwise), **`letter_spacing_px`** (added after each character; the glyphs are then laid one by one from the font's advances) and **a drop shadow** (`shadow_offset_px`, `shadow_color`: the text drawn once in the shadow's colour at the offset, under the outline and the text). The fixture shows each: with `hud/panel.png` beside the scene, a nine-sliced panel behind its `[E] Pick up` prompt, and with `hud/font.ttf`, one line in that font at the top of the picture with a shadow and letter spacing; a scene without the files draws what it drew.

**Where each element stood is in the report** (`game.hud_elements`, and the summary's `interactive.game.hud_elements`): every element, the stack's rows first — `{id, kind: "meter"|"line"|"text", anchor, text, visible, tick, bounds_px: [x0, y0, x1, y1]}`, and for an element of the module's its `align`, `offset_px`, `size_px`, `color`, `outline_px`, `outline_color`, `panel` (and its colour and padding), `font` (and the font's `font_path`), `letter_spacing_px`, `shadow_offset_px` and `shadow_color`; an image is `kind: "image"` with its `path`, `image_px` (the file's own width and height), `size_px`, `slice_px` and `tint` — with the bounds the last frame's layout drew it at, `[0, 0, 0, 0]` when it drew nothing there (hidden, empty). So a game's test asserts that its prompt was on the screen and where, without reading a picture.

**Testing** (`tests/game_view_tests.cpp`, `tests/game_hud_tests.cpp`, `tests/game_props_view_tests.cpp`): with no device, nine elements on the nine anchors of a 1920×1080 layout stand where the anchors and alignments say (the top-left one at (96, 54), the centre one centred on (960, 540)), a hidden one draws nothing, one said again under its id is replaced in place, twice the picture is twice the box, the stack's meter and lines are listed under their reserved ids inside its panel, the 65th element is `OutOfRange`, a tick clears the stack and keeps the elements and a new run clears both; end to end, a state file that stands the fixture's can a metre in front of the walker brings up the prompt from the first tick, and the report lists it centred across an 800×450 picture and 33 px below its middle, beside the stack's rows. The same 120-tick replay captured with the fixture and without differs only where the HUD is — at 800×450 (10 px, the clamp's floor) in 15,206 pixels, every one in the top-left quarter and **none** elsewhere, the rest of the picture the same picture to the byte; at 1600×900 (15 px) in 33,692 pixels from (80, 45) to (430, 140), inside the quarter and past the safe margin; at 320×180 (10 px) from (16, 9) to (249, 73), inside the 16×9 safe margin and short of the region's far edge, so nothing was cut. With no device, the font table (1080 → 18, 2160 at 150 % → 54, 450 → 10, 4320 at 200 % → 64), the HUD and result panels laid out inside the safe region at 320×180, 1600×900 and 3840×2160 at 150 %, and the bake's cost against a same-size layout's. **On HDR, with the game's art** (	ests/game_hud_hdr_view_tests.cpp, 2026-10-19): the fixture's content directory holds a 64×64 stand-in panel the test writes (a 16-pixel opaque white border round a blue at half alpha) and Roboto from the ImGui checkout the build fetched, and the prompt replay is captured at 800×450 in sdr8, hdr10 and scrgb at 200 nits' paper white, against the same replay with a clear panel. In all three the overlay is laid out and the panel (338, 245)–(463, 272), the prompt inside it and the title (311, 23)–(488, 46) have bounds; the panel's white border is light 1.0 of paper white (sdr8 1..1, hdr10 0.996..1.005 under PQ's dither, scrgb 1..1), nothing in the HUD's boxes is past 1.006, 397–434 of the title's pixels reach paper white, the capture's PQ histogram counts 2,364 green channels within a code of 592 (PQ's code for 200 nits), and the panel's half alpha is a blend of the codes on SDR (within 0.43 of a code) and of the light on HDR (within 1.4 % on hdr10, 0.34 % on scrgb); the prompt's outline reaches black. With ENGINE_VIEW_BASELINE naming another build's engine-view (its game/walk_poc beside it), the same replay without the art is captured by both and compared: against the gate's build of 2026-10-19 (136e13fe), 0 bytes differ.

**The summary's `interactive.game` block** (and the report's `game`): `module`, `name`, `build` (the generation), `reloads`, `refused_reloads`, `last_refusal`, `ticks` (calls of `game_tick`), `last_tick`, `actions` (the declared names, by bit), `ended`, `result`, `runs` (this session's, the first included) and `results` (the ended runs' results, in order, when R started another), `error`, `state_file`, `restored`, `restored_session` (whether a version 2 session block was restored), `session` (what the next save writes as that block), `state` (the state as its descriptor writes it), `abi` (the hotload ABI the module declared), `hud` (the last tick's lines and meters, the count of elements and their sets, removes and refusals), `hud_elements` (every element with its bounds, above), `clock` (`sun_time_s`, `world_time_s`, `sky_time_s`, `day_fraction`, `sun_rate`, `rate_changes`, `day_length_s`, `world_rate`, `world_rate_changes`), `placements` (`queries`, `found`, `refused` — radii past `max_radius_m` — `generated` tiles and their `generate_ms`, `world`), `overlay` (the HUD as drawn, [below](#the-hud-provisional)), and `actors`, `actor_calls` and `ground` ([Actors](#actors-the-characters-a-game-moves)).

**Testing.** With no GPU (`tests/game_runner_tests.cpp`, the fixture loaded by the runner and ticked by hand): a second of walking drains 0.25 and the HUD says the meter and the day; `interact` pressed on a tick is read on that tick once; a rebuild of the fixture compiled in the test's scratch directory with a state field added is swapped in by the poll with thirst carried and the new field at its default, and the next second drains from there; an unknown action and a file that is not a library are refused; a module failing at tick 30 ends the run at tick 30 with its line; a saved run nearly out of water ends through `end_run` with its seconds and distance, and the state file reads back in a second runner; a state file of another type is refused naming both; and a placement the host names within reach refills the meter. End to end (`tests/game_view_tests.cpp`, offscreen, over a 128 m window of the committed erg): a 480-tick walk with E pressed once ticks the module 480 times, its metres are the walker's to a millionth, and the report carries the game and the walk; a run with a quarter-second of water left ends through `end_run` long before the recording does, its state file holding the ended run; `--game-state` run twice walks the second on top of the first; the bad-action build is refused before any device, exit 1, the report written; the failing build ends the session at tick 30, in the eighth frame, reason `game_error`; and **a live walk in a window on the test's desktop, injected and recorded with `--game`, replays offscreen to the same module state and trajectory hash**. Over the ruins-walk scene streamed with `--world` (`tests/walk_ruins_view_tests.cpp`), a walker standing beside the building finds the `ruins` within 6 m on every one of its 240 ticks from the first frame (241 queries, 241 found) and drinks the whole time.

## `--report`: what a tester sends back

**One file, written at exit whichever way the run ended** (`report.h`, `report.cpp`; roadmap P21). A build that reaches another person's machine — a packaged game, a bundle of tests — comes back as whatever that person attaches, and someone with no checkout and no idea which console line matters can attach one file. `--report <file.json>` writes it: on success, on a failure the process can still write after (a scene that does not load, a device that is not there, a game module that fails), and after a window was closed or Escape ended the session. It says where it is on stderr.

| Block | What |
|---|---|
| `format`, `version` | `engine.view.report`, 1 |
| `exit` | `code` (the process's), `reason` — `frames` (`--frames` ran out), `session` (a replay's or an injected session's last tick), `path` (a windowed camera path's end), `escape`, `closed` (the window), `end_run` and `game_error` (a `--game` module ended it), `error`, or `done` — and `error`, the first failure's line (`<stage>: <why>`, the words stderr got) |
| `machine` | `adapter`, `driver` (name and version), `vulkan`, `tier` (the device's verdict: `raster`, `rt`, `rt-cluster`), `raster` and `shadows` (what the session drew with), `device` (false when none was made), `os` (the running kernel's version, not the one built for), `cpu` (its brand string), `cpu_baseline` (the binary's: `x86-64-v3` or `x86-64-v2`), and `build` (`commit`, `dirty`) |
| `frames` | `count`, `median_ms`, `p95_ms`, `p99_ms`, `worst_ms`, `resolution_ms`, `width`, `height`: frame start to frame start in a window, `FrameRecord::frame_ms` offscreen |
| `walk` | `walked`, `distance_m`, `seconds`, `drops` — the walker's own stats, for a session that walked |
| `game` | a `--game` module's block (the summary's `game`), or null; with `hud_elements`, every HUD element's last state and the screen bounds the last frame drew it at ([The HUD, provisional](#the-hud-provisional)) |
| `summary` | the summary line the run printed, parsed; null for a run that ended before it had one |

**Why it embeds the summary rather than describing the run again.** The summary is the one writer of what a run measured, and a field it gains should reach a tester's report without anyone remembering a second schema; the report adds only what a summary does not say — the driver and the OS, the spread of a live session's frames when nobody asked for `--benchmark`, and a run that never got as far as a summary. **The frame times cost nothing a frame**: they go into a fixed histogram of 0.1 ms bins to a second (40 KB, allocated when `--report` is parsed), so an hour's session keeps no list, and a percentile is its bin's upper edge (never past the worst frame, which is kept exactly). A real report, from the test below on the RTX 5090 with the machine compiling beside it (the summary cut short):

```json
{"exit":{"code":0,"error":"","reason":"frames"},"format":"engine.view.report",
 "frames":{"count":7,"height":96,"median_ms":8.4,"p95_ms":43.9765,"p99_ms":43.9765,"resolution_ms":0.1,"width":160,"worst_ms":43.9765},
 "game":null,
 "machine":{"adapter":"NVIDIA GeForce RTX 5090","build":{"commit":"5f20ba86…","dirty":true},"cpu":"Intel(R) Core(TM) i9-10980XE CPU @ 3.00GHz",
            "cpu_baseline":"x86-64-v3","device":true,"driver":"NVIDIA 617.42","os":"Windows 10.0.26200","raster":"hw","shadows":"rt","tier":"rt-cluster","vulkan":"1.4.351"},
 "summary":{"frames":8,"…":"…"},"version":1,"walk":{"distance_m":0.0,"drops":0,"seconds":0.0,"walked":false}}
```

**Testing** (`tests/game_view_tests.cpp`): a windowed run of eight frames on the test's desktop writes a report whose exit is `frames`, whose machine block names the adapter, driver, tier, raster mode, OS and baseline, whose frames count seven intervals in order (median ≤ p99, worst ≥ p95) and whose summary is the printed line's eight frames; and an offscreen run of a scene that does not exist exits non-zero and still writes one, its `exit.error` naming the `scene` stage and its summary null.

## package-game: a zip somebody else double-clicks

**`tools/package-game.ps1 -Preset msvc-release -Module <name> -Scene <scene.json> [-Out <zip>] [-Width <w>] [-Height <h>] [-TimeRate <r>] [-SunRate <r>] [-DayLength <s>]`** (roadmap P41) makes the zip a proof of concept reaches other hardware in, on `tools/package-tests.ps1`'s pattern: a machine with no toolchain, no checkout and no PowerShell 7 unzips it, double-clicks `run.cmd`, plays, and sends back the one `report.json` ([above](#--report-what-a-tester-sends-back)). The run script starts the dunes at `-TimeRate` (1, a game second a world second; only where the scene's terrain has dunes) and the sun's day at `-SunRate` (60, a game day in 24 minutes), or at `-DayLength` real seconds a day when that is given (it wins over `-SunRate`; R88), so a tester sees the day turn without the sand racing; `[` `]` step the day's length and `,` `.` the world's rate ([the two rates](#the-two-rates-on-four-keys)). **`-SunRate 60` and `-TimeRate 1` stay the walk_poc's defaults; a sixty-minute day (`-DayLength 3600`) for the next package is the owner's call.** It holds:

| Path | What |
|---|---|
| `engine-view.exe` | the preset's build — statically linked, shaders embedded; the build's `shaders/manifest.json` stays out for package-tests' reason (absolute paths into the build tree and a compiler) |
| `game/<name>.dll` | the game module, `build/<preset>/bin/game/<name>.dll` |
| `content/…` | the scene and every file it names, resolved against the file naming it (a string value of the JSON that is a relative path to an existing file or directory), recursively through the JSON files it names, at their paths under the repository so the scene's own relative paths resolve |
| `run.cmd` | `engine-view --scene <scene> [--world] --walk --game game\<name>.dll --report report.json --ddc ddc --width W --height H`, every path under the folder (`%~dp0`), then where the report is and a pause; `--world` only for a scene that streams (a `world` block or `ruins`), since engine-view refuses it otherwise; extra arguments pass through |
| `README.txt` | what it is, how to run it, the keys of the action map's fifth revision, what to send back, and that it writes nothing outside its folder |
| `package.json` | `engine.game.package` 1: the commit, the preset, the module, the scene, the files with their sizes, the runtime it needs, and `args`, run.cmd's arguments with `{dir}` for the folder (what the test runs) |

**Two layouts, one script** (roadmap P42). The same file runs from an exported game SDK (`<sdk>/tools/package-game.ps1`, `tools/export-game-sdk.ps1`, [game-authoring](../game-authoring.md)), and tells the layouts apart by the SDK's marker, `sdk.json` beside its parent directory: from the engine tree it packs `build/<preset>/bin/engine-view` and `bin/game/<Module>.dll`; from an SDK it packs the SDK's `bin/engine-view`, reads the preset and the commit from `sdk.json`, and takes the module as **`-ModuleDll <file>`** (a game repository's build, its name from the file; `-Module` still names one the SDK carries, such as `walk_poc`). `-ModuleDll` works from the tree too. The scene's content is resolved by `tools/lib/GameContent.psm1`, which the exporter shares: every string value of the scene's JSON that is a relative path to a file beside it, recursively through the JSON files it names. **The scene rule differs by layout**: from the tree the scene must be under the repository's `content/` (a package ships the repository's fixtures and nothing else); from an SDK it may be under the SDK's `content/`, kept at the same `content/…` path, or **anywhere else** — a game repository's own scene — in which case its directory is its root, everything it names must be under that directory, and it is carried at `content/<the directory's name>/…`. `package.json` says which layout packed it (`layout`) and where the module came from (`module_source`).

**What it refuses**: anything under the owner's `game_engine_local` (generated assets and workflow files are never shipped), in either layout, and anything outside the scene's root as above — the scene itself or anything it names — exit 2 with the path; a missing `engine-view` or module, with how to build it. **What a run writes**, all inside the folder: `report.json`, `ddc\` (the derived-data cache, which `--ddc` keeps there rather than in a checkout the far end does not have) and the module host's per-load copies in `game\<name>.dll.hotload\`. The far end needs the Visual C++ 2015–2022 x64 redistributable and a Vulkan driver, as a test bundle does. **Size**: `walk_poc` over `desert-endless` from `msvc-debug` is 7.3 MB zipped, 31 MB unpacked (a 32 MB Debug engine-view, two content files); a release engine-view is a fraction of that.

**Testing** (`tests/game_view_tests.cpp`, `package-game: …`): the script packs this build's engine-view and `walk_poc` over `desert-endless` into the test's scratch directory, refuses a scene there (outside `content/`, exit 2, naming why), and the zip is unpacked with `Expand-Archive` and played with `package.json`'s arguments and `--frames 20` in a window on the test's desktop: exit 0, and `report.json` in the folder with the exit `frames`, the adapter and the OS, nineteen frame intervals, the game `walk_poc` ticked and the summary; the cache is in the folder too. About a minute in `msvc-debug`.

## Material packs: pack-materials and fetch-materials

The default PBR sets a generated mesh's parts fall back to by role ([materials](materials.md)) travel
as a zip, outside the repository, like the Khronos samples:

- **`tools/pack-materials.ps1 -From <staging dir> -Out <zip> [-Name] [-Version] [-Licence]`** packs a
  staging directory laid out `<species>/<part>/` — the five maps (`basecolor.png` or
  `base_color.png`, `normal`, `roughness`, `metallic`, `height`), the lab's `material.json` and a
  `provenance.json` naming the models — into a zip of `pack.json` (per role: the five maps with
  SHA-256 and resolution, `tile_m`, the normal convention and strength, the provenance copied as
  text, the licence line of every model that made it) and `LICENSES.md`. It refuses a missing map,
  a side that is not a power of two, a normal map that is not OpenGL, and a model its licence table
  does not have — StableMaterials (OpenRAIL-M) and PBRify (CC0) today
  ([content generation](../content-generation.md#material-packs)). One JSON line: the zip, its
  SHA-256, the roles.
- **`tools/fetch-materials.ps1 [-Source <zip path or URL>] [-Expect <sha256>] [-Out <dir>]`** unpacks
  one into `content/materials/` (git-ignored but for its README.md): the zip must hash to `-Expect`,
  or to the hash pinned in the script when the source is the pinned default, and every map to what
  `pack.json` says, or nothing is unpacked; the destination's old contents are replaced and its
  README.md kept, and the licence file lands with the maps. **No default is hosted yet**, so with no
  `-Source` it exits 2 saying where to point it.

`engine-view --materials <dir>` names a pack for a run whose cache root is named (the repository's
own is found with the repository's cache otherwise); `package-game` and `export-game-sdk` carry
