`content/materials/` when it is there, the first passing `--materials` to the packaged engine-view
(`package.json`'s `materials`). `tools/materials-pack.Tests.ps1` (`tools.materials_pack`) runs both
scripts over stand-in sets offline.

## What else the machine was doing, in both hosts' numbers

A frame time from this project's development box is not a frame time from an idle machine: it renders beside GPU jobs that hold most of the card and beside several agent builds. So **engine-view's JSON summary and `render.benchmark`'s result both carry the load they were taken under**, in the same shape and against the same thresholds the CPU benchmark harness uses ([bench](bench.md#measuring-on-a-shared-machine)).

Two blocks, both **added** to the existing fields — nothing was renamed and nothing moved out, because tests and scripts read this line:

```json
"gpu_memory":{"budget_mib":31614,"used_mib":297,"device_local_total_mib":32404},
"machine_state":{"start":{"cpu_total_pct":9.3,"cpu_own_pct":0.0,"cpu_others_pct":9.3,
                          "gpu_util_pct":0.0,"gpu_memory_used_mib":17117,
                          "gpu_memory_total_mib":32607,"session_locked":false,
                          "gpu_lock":null},
                 "end":{...}}
```

`gpu_lock` is the machine-wide GPU lock as the sample found it — `null` when nobody held it, else `{"owner","purpose","expires","mine","expired"}` ([bench](bench.md#measuring-on-a-shared-machine), "The GPU lock") — and a lock somebody else held is a WARNING of its own. It is in engine-view's summary only: the protocol's `MachineState` schema type does not carry it yet, so over `render.benchmark` and `render.evaluate` the lock shows in engine-host's stderr WARNING and not in the result. A host started under a lock-holding wrapper (`gpu-lock.ps1 run`, `gpu-smoke.ps1`, `reference-compare.ps1`) inherits `ENGINE_GPU_LOCK_HOLDER` and reports the lock as `"mine":true` rather than warning about its own parent.

`gpu_memory` is the renderer's ([renderer](renderer.md)), read from the Vulkan device around the run. `machine_state` is sampled **outside** the timed region — before the first frame and after the last — because the sampler sleeps for a quarter of a second to get a CPU percentage and spawns `nvidia-smi` for the GPU reading, and neither belongs between two timed frames. Every field is `null` where the platform could not answer, so "quiet" and "not measured" are different answers; no process names are ever reported.

**Read the two together.** In the line above the Vulkan budget says 31,614 MiB of 32,404 MiB were available to this process while `nvidia-smi` says 17,117 MiB of the card were in use: the budget does not fully see a CUDA tenant, so `device_local_total_mib - budget_mib` is a lower bound on what others hold and `gpu_memory_used_mib` in `machine_state` is the number that answers "is somebody else on this GPU".

When other processes used more than 10% of the CPU or the GPU was more than 20% busy at either end of the run, both hosts write **one WARNING line to stderr** — the same sentence `foundation/bench` prints, for the same reason: stdout is the summary and the protocol, and a caveat that corrupted either would be worse than no caveat. engine-view writes it **before** the summary line and then flushes the summary whole, so the summary is the last line of the two streams merged and never has the caveat spliced into it: stdout into a pipe is fully buffered, and until 2026-09-24 the summary went into the buffer first and out at exit, which only happened to put it last — and split any summary longer than the buffer, which an interactive session's is, around the caveat. It does not mean the numbers are wrong; it means they are upper bounds, and a write-up that quotes them owes its reader the "Machine state" line ([docs/experiments/README.md](../experiments/README.md)).

`render.benchmark`'s result went to `@version(2)` for this, and `RenderStats` with it: `stats.gpu_memory` and `machine_state` are `@since(2)` with defaults, so an older client reading the result sees exactly the fields it saw before.

**Multi-view over the protocol.** `RenderSettings` went to `@version(2)` for this: `views` (`single`, `surround3`, `panini`), `side_yaw_deg`, `panini_d` and `peripheral_lod` are `@since(2)` with the single-view defaults, so both hosts spell the layout the same way and parse it with the same function ([settings.h](renderer.md#public-api)). `RenderStats` went to `@version(3)` and gained `views` — one `RenderViewStats` per view with its rectangle, its source extent, its tier, its visible pairs and its milliseconds by pass — beside `view_layout` and `oversample`. Changing the layout between calls rebuilds the GPU scene and the renderer the way any other settings change does, because the per-frame working set is sized by the view count. This is the path E9 measured through, since it renders offscreen at 11520×2160 on a machine whose window would not be that big.

**Where there is no GPU**, every `render.*` method fails with error code **1007 `RenderUnavailable`** and a message naming the adapter and what it lacks — no Vulkan loader or driver, no 64-bit buffer atomics, or, for `"raster":"rt"`, an explicit `"shadows":"rt"` or the reference integrator, the sentence the adapter's own verdict carries about ray tracing (`renderer::unavailable_reason`), which names the missing extension: on the Pascal server, "NVIDIA TITAN Xp cannot trace rays: no VK_KHR_ray_query and no VK_NV_cluster_acceleration_structure: …". A device without mesh shaders is not unavailable: `"raster":"hw"` resolves to the vertex path there, and the scene info says `"raster":"vertex"`. It is a code of its own so a client can tell "this machine cannot render" from "you asked for something wrong" ([protocol](protocol.md)); the end-to-end test skips on it exactly as engine-view's suite skips on exit 3, which is what lets one suite run on the hosted CI runners and on the GPU machines alike.

## The console: Luau scripts over the protocol

`engine-host --console` runs **one Luau script against the host's own dispatcher** instead of serving JSON-RPC on stdin, and `engine-cli --console <file>` is the same thing as one line for an agent at a terminal (plan 06 §6.3's third interface, [status note](../plan/06-agent-tooling.md#63-mcp-bridge); roadmap A33). It exists because a coding agent on the local machine does more with fewer round trips through a script it writes and runs against the live engine than through one tool call at a time: a script composes, loops, branches and keeps local state, so "create forty props on a ring, capture each, keep the ones whose coverage is above a threshold" is one file and one run, not two hundred tool calls with the whole conversation re-sent each time. The owner runs his own business software this way, with a Lua console that Claude Code drives.

```
engine-host --console [--script <file.luau>] [--step-budget <n>] [--memory-mb <n>]
            [--roles <file.json>] [--actor <name>] [--role <name>] [--task <id>] [--mount ...] [-- <script args>...]
engine-cli  [--host <path>] [--roles <file>] [--actor <name>] [--role <name>] [--task <id>] [--step-budget <n>] [--memory-mb <n>]
            --console <file.luau | -> [-- <script args>...]
```

- **Where the script comes from.** `--script <file>`, or stdin until EOF without it; engine-cli's `-` hands its own stdin over. Everything after `--` is the script's `...` (`local dir = ...`). engine-cli refuses a method, `--doc` or `--report` beside `--console`: the script opens its own documents, and a path it needs is an argument.
- **What a script can reach.** Every method the host serves, two ways: `engine.call("doc.apply", {...})`, and one generated function per method named by the catalogue — `engine.doc.apply{...}`, `engine.render.capture{...}`, `engine.session.open{...}`, and `engine.ping()`, `engine.methods()` for the `engine.*` ones. `engine.json(value [, pretty])` turns a result into JSON text, since `print` shows a table as its address. `print` is the script's own output, on stdout, a line at a time (engine-cli forwards it as the host writes it). The Luau sandbox is the scripting module's: no `io`, `os`, `debug`, `require` or `loadstring`, so a script reaches the file system only through methods, as any client does.
- **Every call is a request.** A call is a JSON-RPC request object handed to the same `protocol::Dispatcher` a line on stdin reaches, so attribution, roles and the journal hold exactly as for any client: the blanks of a call's `attribution` are `--actor`, `--role` and `--task`; with `--roles` the role gate refuses a write the role does not name (1008) and never a read, by the [read-only rule](protocol.md#identity-and-what-a-role-restricts); a `doc.apply` is a journaled transaction with its rationale. **The console mutates only by calling methods** — the sanctioned path — and is a client, not a gameplay script ([scripting](scripting.md#programs-the-console)).
- **Params and results.** A params table is read through the method's params type in the schema registry, never by a binding per method: the descriptors say that `channels = {}` is an empty array and `properties = {}` an empty map, which Luau alone cannot tell apart; a field the type does not have, or a value of the wrong type, reaches the protocol's params reader and comes back as its -32602 with the path. A result is an ordinary table the script may change and send back.
- **Errors.** A method that fails raises a Luau error whose value is a table, `{method, code, message, data}` — `pcall` returns it, so `local ok, err = pcall(engine.doc.apply, p); if not ok and err.code == 1008 then ... end` reads the protocol's code and `err.data.reason` without parsing text. Uncaught, it ends the run.
- **Limits.** One step budget for the whole run (`--step-budget`, default 100,000,000 Luau safepoints, 0 for none): it counts the script's own loop iterations, calls and returns — deterministically, and never the time a method takes — so `while true do end` stops in about a second of a debug build and a render that takes a minute does not. A `pcall` cannot catch its way past it. The heap is `--memory-mb` (default 512).
- **Exit codes.** `0` the script ran to its end. `1` it failed: a compile error, an uncaught error (a method's included) or a limit, reported on stderr as `<chunk>:<line>: <message>` the way a compiler reports a position — `content/console/forbidden_write.luau:30: doc.apply: error 1008: ... {"reason":"method",...}` — and for a limit a second line naming the flag that sets it. `2` the console could not start: a script that does not read, a console flag without `--console`, or a build without the scripting capability (`msvc-minimal`: `--console needs the scripting capability ... (ENGINE_WITH_SCRIPTING=OFF)`). engine-cli exits with the host's code. The host's stdout protocol mode is untouched: without `--console` nothing changes.

**A worked example**, what an agent would write to put a row of objects into a document, read back what it did, and take the last one out again (`engine-cli --console row.luau -- ./world`):

```lua
local dir = ...
local s = engine.session.open({ path = dir, create = true }).session
local who = { actor = "claude", role = "environment", task = "row-of-markers" }

local ids = {}
for i = 1, 8 do
  local id = string.format("000000000000001000000000%08x", i)
  who.rationale = "marker " .. i .. " of a row of eight"
  local r = engine.doc.apply({ session = s, attribution = who, commands = {
    { kind = "CreateObject", id = id, type = "engine.content.AssetProvenance" },
    { kind = "SetProperty", id = id, name = "generator", value = "row-" .. i },
  } })
  assert(r.committed, engine.json(r))
  ids[#ids + 1] = id
end

local listed = engine.doc.objects({ session = s })
print("objects", listed.total)
local ok, err = pcall(engine.doc.undo, { session = s })
print(ok and "undid the last marker" or tostring(err))
print(engine.json(engine.doc.journal({ session = s, offset = 6 }).patches[1].attribution))
```

The fixtures under `content/console/` are four more, each with its command line at the top ([README](../../content/console/README.md)). `apps/engine_cli/tests/console_tests.cpp` runs them end to end: a script that creates a document, applies two transactions with rationales, reads the journal, undoes one and lists objects, asserted in Luau, through engine-cli's one-shot form and again from stdin; the catalogue's generated names, a runtime and a compile error with their chunk and line, and the usage errors; a params table read through its schema type (an empty `properties` map, a wrong type refused as -32602, a function refused before the host sees it); the `qa` role refused on the write with the refusal reaching `pcall`, and uncaught failing at the line of the call; `runaway.luau` stopped by `--step-budget 100000` with exit 1 and the budget named; and a frame captured through `engine.render.capture` with the file on disk, skipped on a machine with no usable GPU. The VM side is `foundation/scripting`'s `ScriptContext::run_program` ([scripting](scripting.md#programs-the-console)), linked into engine-host when the configuration has the scripting capability (ADR-0027), with `ENGINE_HOST_SCRIPTING` for `console.cpp`.

**When to use which** (the three interfaces of [plan 06 §6.3](../plan/06-agent-tooling.md#63-mcp-bridge)): MCP (`engine-mcp`) is the cognitive interface — curated, documented tools with pagination and error hints, for an agent that reasons a step at a time or cannot run local programs; the native protocol (`engine-host --stdio`, `engine-cli <method>`) is the mechanical one — one method a call, for the editor, tests and other programs; the console is the scriptable one — for a coding agent on this machine with a terminal, when the work is many calls that depend on each other's results. **Not yet:** a script cannot keep a host alive between runs (each run is its own host, as engine-cli's calls are, so a loaded scene or a runtime world lasts one script), there is no interactive REPL (stdin is read to EOF, then run), no Luau type-definition file for the method functions, and no subscription or event callback — a script polls `session.events` with its cursor.
## engine-host --listen: the protocol over a loopback WebSocket

```
engine-host --listen 127.0.0.1:0 --doc <dir> [--create] --actor <name>
{"listen":"ws://127.0.0.1:52341/","session":"s1","shell":"http://127.0.0.1:52341/?token=...&session=s1","token":"..."}
```

**One command opens the shell.** The host prints that line first, before it serves; open the `shell` URL in a browser and the page is the [engine shell](engine_shell.md), speaking the protocol back over the same port. The browser is the user's: nothing here starts one. `--doc <dir>` opens a session at start exactly as `session.open` would (through the dispatcher; `--create` creates the document when the directory holds none), and its id is in the line and the URL; without `--doc` a client opens its own. `--shell <dir>` names the shell's files when they are not at `<bin>/../shell`, where the build copies `apps/engine_shell/` (beside `shaders/manifest.json`). The host serves until its standard input closes, as it always has — stdin is still a protocol stream beside the socket — so Ctrl+C, or closing the pipe, ends it.

**What it is** ([protocol](protocol.md#transport) has the transport's rules, [net](net.md) the module): JSON-RPC 2.0 over an RFC 6455 WebSocket, one request per text message, through the same dispatcher and the same lock as stdin (`protocol::Listener`, the protocol's own since 2026-10-11; `listen.cpp` is the host's captures it serves). Loopback only: `--listen 0.0.0.0:...` or any other host exits 2 with a sentence naming the listener rule, before anything binds; `tools/ci/check-no-listeners.ps1` runs the host with `--listen 127.0.0.1:0` from a fresh path. A 128-bit token, printed once, is required on every upgrade (`?token=`, or `X-Engine-Token`), and a connection without it is closed with 1008 and a reason; a browser's upgrade must come from the listener's own origin and every request must name the listener's own address in `Host`. That is loopback and a shared secret, not authentication of a user.

**What it serves over HTTP, and nothing else.** `GET /` is the shell's `index.html` and `GET /shell/<file>` its other files, with the right content types, no directory listing and no path that leaves the directory (`net::safe_relative_path`, before the file is opened). `GET /capture/<id>` is the colour PNG of a capture this process kept, and `GET /capture/<id>/ids` its id buffer as raw little-endian `u32`, three a pixel (instance, cluster, triangle: `renderer::k_id_words`, the layout `<name>.ids.bin` has on disk) — both need the token, since a capture is the world's content. Every other path is a 404 and every other method a 405.

**`render.load {"session": <id>}`** (`RenderLoadParams.session`, exactly one of `mesh`, `scene` and `session`): the session's live `engine.scene.MeshPlacement` records as the scene, through [doc_scene](doc_scene.md) with the document's directory as the path base — the bridge engine-editor's viewport draws through. Loading the **same session again** answers with the same scene id after reading the document's change feed from the revision the scene shows: a placement whose transform changed is placed again in place (in the host's `SceneData`, so a later rebuild for new settings or size starts from where it stands, and in the GPU scene when one exists), anything else — a placement created, deleted or pointed at another mesh — reads the document into the same scene id again. `RenderSceneInfo` says which (`moved`, `rebuilt`) and which revision it now shows. On such a scene `render.capture` answers `records`, instance to record id (the null id for the stand-in ground of a document with no placements), so a client reads an instance out of the ids channel and has the record without knowing the instance order.

**The camera a capture was framed with** (roadmap A32). Every `render.capture` result carries `camera` (`RenderFramedCamera`): `position` and `target` as world positions, `fov_deg`, `znear`, and the look's `yaw_deg` and `pitch_deg` in engine-view's fly convention (forward = (-cos p sin y, sin p, -cos p cos y)) — whether the call named a camera or the host framed it. The first four given back as `camera` draw the same bytes: `fov_deg` is chosen so that its radians are the frame's own float, because the plain conversion can come back a step wide. engine-mcp writes the result as `<name>.json`, so the file carries it too.

**`render.capture` with `keep`** keeps the colour PNG and the id buffer in the host's memory under the result's `capture` id ("c1", "c2", ...), the last eight of them, for the listener to serve; with an empty `out_dir` it writes nothing to disk. That is how the shell shows a picture without ever holding a file path.

**Events** (2026-10-11, roadmap A2; [protocol](protocol.md#events) has the rules). A connection may `session.subscribe` to the session's `journal`, `scene` and `session` events and is then pushed each as a JSON-RPC notification on its own connection, in order, after the change is committed: every commit, undo and redo whoever made it — a second connection, stdin, an attached engine-mcp — and, from `render.load {session}`, `event.scene` when the session's scene moved or was rebuilt, so a second client showing it knows to capture again. A subscriber that stops reading is queued up to `protocol.events.queue` events (256) and then dropped and counted, never waited on. stdin cannot subscribe: it is refused with 1006 and told to poll `session.info`.

**Tests.** `apps/engine_host/tests/transport_tests.cpp` (CTest `engine_host`) drives the real executable through foundation/net's client: the token's refusal and its header form, a foreign origin refused, a fragmented 200 KB `doc.apply` with pings between its frames, a journal entry over the socket byte for byte the one stdin writes, a role refused over the socket with stdin's own error, two connections and stdin answered together, three non-loopback addresses refused with nothing announced, the static files and their guard (`..`, `%2e%2e`, an empty name, a foreign `Host`), and on a GPU `render.load {session}` with two cubes — records in id order, both cubes in the ids, the reported camera given back drawing the same PNG, a transform edit moving in place under the same scene id and changing the picture, a new placement rebuilding into it. `tools/host-ws.Tests.ps1` (CTest `engine_host.host_ws`) is the second, independent client — .NET's `ClientWebSocket` — over the same host, so that a framing bug the in-tree server and client shared could not hide: the token's 1008, a 200 KB request in 51 fragments, a 205 KB answer in one message (the 64-bit length), two connections, an `event.journal` pushed to the second for the first's commit, the close handshake's echo, `GET /`, and the exit when stdin closes. The events have their own cases in `transport_tests.cpp`: a second connection's journal events for a commit, a commit, an undo and a redo in order, a lease's session events, stdio's refusal, unsubscribing, a subscriber that never reads (the bounded queue and its `dropped` count), and on a GPU the scene's events after a move and a rebuild.
## engine-editor: the engine's window on a document

```powershell
build/msvc-debug/bin/engine-editor --doc ./world [--create] [--actor marc] [--width 1600 --height 900]   # the editor
build/msvc-debug/bin/engine-editor --doc ./world --embedded --frames 8 --pick center   # what the embedded test drives: exit 3 without WebView2
build/msvc-debug/bin/engine-editor --doc ./world --no-shell                             # the viewport alone in the window
build/msvc-debug/bin/engine-editor --doc ./world --offscreen --frames 3 --capture editor.png   # the frame and its overlay
build/msvc-debug/bin/engine-editor --doc ./world --no-shell --frames 6 --pick 160,110 --move 0,2,0          # what the window test drives
build/msvc-debug/bin/engine-editor --doc ./world --no-shell --frames 12 --pick 160,110 --drag 160,75 160,45  # a gizmo drag, one transaction
build/msvc-debug/bin/engine-editor --doc ./world --embedded --width 1280 --height 720 --frames 8 --resize-to 800,600   # the layout test: wide, then narrow
build/msvc-debug/bin/engine-editor --doc ./world --embedded --frames 34 --pick center --move 0,0.25,0 --look 90,-45 --test-keys  # focus, keys, the look gesture
build/msvc-debug/bin/engine-editor --doc ./world --embedded --frames 40 --test-page --test-relayout-at 10 --test-drop-socket-at 20  # the page's own checks
build/msvc-debug/bin/engine-editor --doc ./world --no-shell --frames 14 --pick 160,120 --rotate y,90 --test-drop-file crate.glb  # a turn, then a drop
build/msvc-debug/bin/engine-editor --doc ./world --embedded --frames 14 --test-look-os 90,0       # the look through the platform's queue
build/msvc-debug/bin/engine-editor --doc ./world --trace-input look.jsonl                         # every pointer event, for a gesture that misbehaves
build/msvc-debug/bin/engine-editor --version                                                     # the build stamp, one JSON line
```

**What the window is** (roadmap A37, after A34 and A36's spike, toward A24). **One native window holding two children that tile and never overlap**: the web shell ([engine_shell](engine_shell.md#embedded-in-the-editor) — outliner, properties with editing, journal with undo and redo, the command line, the status strip) in the platform's own web view ([webview](webview.md): Edge WebView2 on Windows), and the renderer's **live viewport** in a child window placed over the page's viewport cell and raised above it ([window](window.md#child-windows)). The viewport is the engine's half of the editor: the renderer's picture of a document's placements at the display's rate, engine-view's fly camera (hold the right button over the picture to look, WASD and E/Q to move, Shift faster), **picking** (a left click selects the record under the pointer; the selection is outlined by its box), a **translate gizmo** on the selection, and **undo, redo and delete** on the keys while it has the keyboard. Nothing is composited between the two and nothing crosses per frame: the viewport presents into its own swapchain at vsync as engine-view does, the page repaints when its state changes, and what they share is state over the protocol and the cell's rectangle when it moves. A click in the viewport gives it the keyboard; a click in the page takes it back. What is drawn *in* the viewport — the selection's box, the gizmo, the last refused edit — is Dear ImGui through the renderer's overlay pass ([ADR-0054](../adr/0054-the-tools-ui-is-dear-imgui-drawn-by-the-renderer.md): ImGui is for the tools inside the engine's window); the **three ImGui panels** of A34 (outliner, properties, journal, and the toolbar) **are gone**, retired for the shell's, which the owner judged clearly better ([06 §6.13](../plan/06-agent-tooling.md#613-human-developer-tooling)).

**Why this shape.** A36's spike put the shell in a browser against engine-host and drew its viewport as captures — every frame a render, a readback, a PNG, a fetch and a decode — and the owner's verdict (2026-10-11) was that the panels were right and the slideshow could not be flown. A live viewport inside the page would mean compositing the engine's frames into the browser's every frame (a shared texture, a frame of latency, a sync on each one); tiling does not: the page's layout decides where the cell is, the native child window sits exactly there, and the only traffic is a rectangle when a split moves. The cost is the seams stage 2 owns — the frame a resize takes the child to follow, popups that must stay out of the cell — and none of it touches the frame loop.

**One host, in process.** Both halves are clients of **one host**: the editor runs the protocol's own listener (`protocol::Listener`, [protocol](protocol.md#transport)) in process over a dispatcher round the session it already holds — the built-in methods every host has, plus `editor.layout`, `editor.select` and `editor.state` ([protocol](protocol.md#the-editors-methods)) — on 127.0.0.1 with a fresh token, serving the same `apps/engine_shell/` files from `<bin>/../shell`, and the web view opens the shell from it with `&embedded=1`. One document, one session, one process: the shell's `doc.apply` and the gizmo's are two transactions on the same session with the same attribution (the editor's actor, the director), and the viewport follows the document after each one, whoever made it. Every request runs under the listener's lock, which the editor's frame takes too for everything that reads the document, the selection or the editor's state (events, the scripted gestures, the layout, the viewport's sync, the overlay), and releases for the frame's submission and present — so a request waits at most a frame, and nothing in the editor is touched by two threads. **The selection is an event** ([protocol](protocol.md#events)): every change of it — a click in the viewport, a scripted `--pick`, `editor.select`, a delete — goes through `EditorDocument::select`, which publishes `event.selection` to the session's subscribers, so the shell's outliner follows a pick as it happens rather than at its next half-second poll of `editor.state`, which is gone; the editor's journal events are the session's own, so an agent's edit through an attached engine-mcp reaches the shell the same way. The listener binds loopback only and prints its line first on stdout, as engine-host's does, so a browser (or any client) can be pointed at the same editor: `--listen 127.0.0.1:<port>` fixes the port, `--shell <dir>` the files.

**The first look** (2026-10-11, A38). Nobody had seen the window until a scratch PowerShell script captured it (`System.Drawing` over the window's rectangle, the window from the process): at 1280 × 720 on this machine's 150 % display the page is 853 CSS pixels wide, under the three columns' 950, so the properties column and the title bar's Undo, Redo and connection pill were off the right edge, the viewport cell ran 100 pixels past the client area with the native child clipped by the window, and the command line's fixed 210 CSS pixels left the viewport a 957 × 242 strip. The narrow layout and the clamp below are the answer; embedded, the command line now takes a quarter of the column by default. The picture itself, the dark backing and the panels beside the live viewport looked as intended.

**The narrow layout.** Below 960 CSS pixels of width the page is two columns — the outliner over the properties, the journal a drawer under them, the viewport over the command line ([engine_shell](engine_shell.md#the-narrow-layout)) — so at 150 % any window under 1440 pixels gets it, and an 800 × 600 window at 100 % too; the viewport cell is one rectangle in both layouts and `editor.layout` reports it as before, with a `narrow` flag the editor records. **The child never extends past the client area**: every placement is clamped to the window's client size (`place_viewport`, `editor_methods.h`), and a clamp of more than the page's own rounding (its viewport is whole CSS pixels, so a cell at the edge of a 1280-pixel window at 1.5 reaches 1281) says so in a `warn` line from the `editor` category with the rectangle asked for and the client size. A resize of the window clamps the child into the new client area at once, from the last layout, before the page's own layout for the new size arrives.

**Display scale changes at run time.** The page reports its device pixel ratio with each layout, and the window has its own scale. When the display's scale under the window changes — the window dragged to a monitor of another scale, or the setting changed — SDL says so (`EventKind::ScaleChanged`, [window](window.md#the-displays-scale-and-events-through-the-platforms-queue)), and the editor **re-places the child at once** from the last layout at the ratio scaled by the new scale over the old (`rescaled_layout`) and **asks the page for a fresh layout** with `editor.relayout`, a notification over the listener's socket to every connection ([protocol](protocol.md#the-editors-methods)), which the page answers whether or not its rectangle moved. Picking follows by itself: the pointer's scale is read from the child's size against the swapchain's every frame, and the swapchain follows the child's new size. (WebView2 tracks its monitor's scale itself, so the page usually sends the new layout unasked; the request is for the case where it did not, and it costs one message.)

**Keyboard focus** moves between the page and the viewport by click, as between any two controls: a press in the viewport gives its child window the keyboard (`Window::focus`), and a click in the page gives it back to the web view, whose keys never reach SDL at all. So **Ctrl+Z in the viewport is the editor's undo** and **Ctrl+Z in the page is the page's own** — its Undo button's `doc.undo` through the session, one step, never two. The top-level window's own keys (after Alt+Tab Windows gives the keyboard back to the top-level window, not to a child) are the viewport's.

**The look gesture on a real pointer** (2026-10-12, A39). The owner found that holding the right button over the viewport turned the camera "a tiny bit, then it stops", while WASD and clicks worked and the `--look` test passed. The test pushes its events into SDL's queue as the child's own, past everything a real pointer goes through; `--trace-input <file.jsonl>` (kept: one line per pointer event the window gives the editor — kind, position, delta, SDL's timestamp, whether it was the child's, and what SDL and the platform said at that moment about relative mode, keyboard and mouse focus, the cursor and the child's rectangle — and one per frame with the camera's yaw and pitch after its ticks, written when the run ends) over a real pointer driven by a scratch `SendInput` script showed that **no motion at all reached the editor between the press and the release**: SDL runs relative mode only for its keyboard focus, which an adopted child never is, and drops the absolute motion of a window flagged for it, so the motion before the flag went on was all there was. The fix is the window module's ([window](window.md#child-windows), "Relative mode on an adopted child"): a child's relative mode is its own, on the platform's pointer messages — cursor hidden, confined to the viewport, deltas read from the child's pointer and the cursor put back where the press was, and shown there when the button comes up. The editor also asks for the mode on a change only. Measured on the owner's desktop at 22:02 UTC on 2026-10-12 with nobody at it (a 1280 × 720 debug editor, `--frames 480`): 300 counts of `SendInput` motion to the right turned the camera 0° before the fix (21:50, zero motion events, the cursor left the viewport) and 60.25° after (30 events, 478 cursor pixels, exactly the fly camera's 0.0022 rad a pixel; about 20° per 100 counts at this machine's pointer speed), the cursor held inside the viewport throughout and back at the press point at the release. The deltas are cursor pixels after the system's pointer acceleration, where engine-view's top-level window reads raw counts.

**The web view's profile** (its cache and the page's local storage, where the splits are remembered) is the document's: `<doc>/.editor/webview/`, in a directory that ignores itself — `<doc>/.editor/.gitignore` is `*`, written the first time, so a document kept in git never picks up a browser cache whatever the repository's own rules say. `--webview-profile <dir>` names another. Before A38 it was `%APPDATA%\engine-editor.exe`, shared by every editor and every test on the machine ([webview](webview.md)). A test's document is inside its `TempDir`, so its profile is too.

**Where there is no web view** — a build without `ENGINE_WITH_WEBVIEW` (the minimal build, every platform but Windows), a machine without the WebView2 runtime — the window is the viewport alone and the editor says so on stderr; the listener still serves, so the printed `shell` URL opens the same shell in a browser beside it. `--embedded` asks for the web view or nothing: it exits 3 with the reason, which is how the embedded test skips on a hosted runner. `--no-shell` is the viewport alone with no listener (unless `--listen` asks for one), which the window tests that do not need a web view use. `--offscreen` is the viewport alone with no window, as before.

**The gizmo, and one command per gesture.** Three handles run from the selection box's centre along world x (red), y (green) and z (blue), drawn on the overlay with the frame's own view-projection, the one the box is projected with, and 80 window units long at the centre's depth whatever the selection's size or the display's scale. **Hover is a CPU hit test**, never a read of the visibility buffer: the pointer against the three projected segments with a 6-unit tolerance, past a dead zone round the centre where all three meet (`viewport_math.h`, `hover_axis`), and the handle under the pointer is lit. A press on a handle starts a drag instead of a pick. While the button is held the drag **only previews**: the pointer's ray through the frame's inverse view-projection meets the axis line through the box's centre (`pixel_ray`, `closest_on_axis`), the distance along the axis since the press is the delta (`AxisDrag`, in the world, so flying while dragging does not move the selection), and the instance moves in the viewport alone — `Viewport::preview`, which is `SceneRenderer::move_instances`, once a frame (`EditorInput::flush`, because each call waits for the device and a mouse sends hundreds of moves a second) — with **no document write**. Releasing applies **one** `doc.apply` holding **one** `cmd_set(id, "translation", [x, y, z])` through `EditorDocument::apply`, attributed to the editor's caller with the rationale `move <name> along <axis>`; the new translation is the old one plus the delta on the dragged axis in f64, the other two components untouched to the bit. The viewport then follows the transaction through `Viewport::sync` exactly as it follows anyone's — a placement whose transform changed is moved in place — so a drag and the same translation set over the protocol by somebody else end in the same state. **Escape** during a drag puts the preview back and writes nothing; so does a release that moved nothing, and a drag the window loses focus in the middle of. An axis pointing at the eye cannot be dragged (within a degree of the line of sight the pointer says nothing about where along it it is), and a press on it does nothing.

**The rotate gizmo** (2026-10-12, A39), in the move's shape. **T** is the move tool and **R** the rotate tool while the viewport has the keyboard (W/A/S/D/E/Q are the camera's); the shell's title bar has a Move/Rotate toggle that calls `editor.tool`, and the status strip shows which ([engine_shell](engine_shell.md#embedded-in-the-editor)); a change made either way reaches every page as an `editor.tool` notification. **Three rings** about world x (red), y (green) and z (blue) stand at the **placement's origin** — the point a change of the record's `rotation` turns it about, since the mesh's fit goes through the rotation (`renderer::instance_translation`); for a mesh the fit centres, that is the box's centre where the handles stand — 70 window units in radius at its depth, drawn as 64-segment polylines with the frame's own view (a segment only where both ends are in front of the eye). **Hover is a CPU hit test**, the distance to each ring's projected polyline (`hover_ring`, viewport_math.h), and **y is the primary ring**: wherever it is within reach it wins, otherwise the nearest. **A drag** turns the selection about the ring's axis by the angle, in the ring's plane, between where the pointer's ray met the plane at the press and where it meets it now, summed step by step so a gesture can go past half a turn (`RingDrag`), worked out in f64 in the world about the pivot so the eye's place rounds nothing — the same world line from an eye flown three metres gives the same angle to the bit — and a plane seen within a degree of edge-on is refused, as `closest_on_axis` refuses an axis along the line of sight. **Shift snaps** to 15° steps. **One gesture, one command**: the preview turns the instance through `Viewport::preview` (a rotation is a move, not a rebuild); the release applies one `doc.apply` of one `cmd_set(id, "rotation", [x, y, z, w])` with the rationale `rotate <name> about <axis> by <N>°`. The new `rotation` is the record's own composed with the turn, `yaw_deg` left as the author wrote it: the placement stands at Y R (`yaw_deg` is applied after `rotation`, doc_scene.md), turned it is G Y R, so the record's `rotation` becomes Y⁻¹ G Y R — G R about y, which the yaw commutes with — in f64, normalised and in one canonical sign (w > 0, or w = 0 and the first non-zero component positive, no negative zero), so a quarter turn and back, or four quarter turns, give back the bits they started from (`turned_rotation`). Escape restores the preview; a turn that snaps or comes to nothing writes nothing; Ctrl+Z undoes it as a move. The properties panel shows `rotation` as its four numbers and, beside them, yaw, pitch and roll in degrees.

**Adding assets** (2026-10-12, A39). A document names a mesh by a path relative to its directory (`MeshPlacement.mesh`), so a mesh is added by **copying it into the document** and placing it: `editor.import_mesh {path, name?, at?}` ([protocol](protocol.md#the-editors-methods)) checks the file (`.gltf`, `.glb` or `.clusters`; the loader's own verdict on it), copies it — and a `.gltf`'s external buffers and images, resolved from the file, which must sit in its directory or below — into `<doc>/assets/<stem>/` (refusing to overwrite other bytes, reusing the same bytes), then applies one transaction creating the placement named after the file, with the rationale `import <file>`, where the viewport's line of sight meets the ground within 50 m (else 10 m in front of the camera) unless `at` says; the new record is selected and the viewport rebuilds with it through the derived-data cache. Every check runs before the first byte is written, so a refusal leaves the document and its directory as they were. Two ways in: the shell's **Add…** button in the outliner's header, which asks the editor for a file through `editor.pick_file` — the platform's own open-file dialog, on the editor's thread with the dispatch lock released, a dialog a person asked for in a desktop tool and never an error dialog; it is never shown with a test flag, and the method answers null instead — and **a file dropped on the viewport** (`EventKind::DropFile`, [window](window.md#child-windows)), which the editor imports through the same method on its own dispatcher; a refusal is logged with the reason. What is not done: the ground under the viewport's centre is the plane y = 0, not the picked surface (that needs the depth the pick does not read back), and a drop on the page is not an import (a browser's drop gives no path).

**Undo, redo, delete.** Ctrl+Z undoes and Ctrl+Y (or Ctrl+Shift+Z) redoes, through the session (`EditorDocument::undo`/`redo`, one step each, `doc.undo`/`doc.redo`) while the viewport has the keyboard; the shell's Undo and Redo buttons (and its own Ctrl+Z) are `doc.undo`/`doc.redo` on the same session over the listener, and the viewport follows them the same way. **Delete** removes the selected record through one `doc.apply` with `cmd_delete` and the rationale `delete <name>`, and selects nothing. Each is one step or one transaction and the journal shows it; the viewport follows — an undone move is a move, an undone create or delete is a rebuild. **Ctrl is the editor's modifier here**, so unlike in engine-view it does not lower the camera (Q does). A refused edit does not end the run: it is drawn in the viewport's top-left corner.

**Picking done right.** The window reports the pointer in its own coordinates and the picture is the swapchain's pixels; under display scaling (150 %: a 1280 × 720 window is 1920 × 1080 pixels) the two differ and a click would land beside its target. Every pointer event is multiplied by the swapchain's size over the window's logical size (`Window::logical_size`, [window](window.md); `pointer_scale` in `viewport_math.h`) before ImGui, the gizmo or a pick sees it, so ImGui, the handles and the visibility buffer all work in the same pixels. `--pick` and `--drag` take **window coordinates** and go through the same scaling (offscreen, and wherever nothing scales, they are the picture's pixels). A pick still reads the whole visibility buffer back for one pixel (`Viewport::pick`, `SceneRenderer::read_last_frame`): right for a click and wrong for anything per frame, which is why hover does not use it, and the summary's **`pick_ms`** says what the last one cost (in a debug build, 14 ms in the 320 × 240 window test and 40 to 65 ms offscreen at 640 × 400, with other agents building on the machine: upper bounds, not costs).

**The viewport talks to the engine only through the protocol's own objects**, because a feature reachable only from the GUI is a protocol bug ([06 §6.13](../plan/06-agent-tooling.md#613-human-developer-tooling)): the document is opened by `protocol::SessionManager`, the object engine-host's `session.open` makes, and the same manager is what the editor's dispatcher serves; every edit the viewport makes is a command list through `Session::apply`'s **checked** form with the editor's `protocol::Caller` (actor `--actor`, role `director` — the human is the director, 06 §6.10 — task `editor`, a rationale per transaction), exactly what `doc.apply` does; undo and redo are the session's. `EditorDocument` still builds the outliner, property and journal models the ImGui panels drew (and its tests still hold them), which are what the shell reads over the protocol as `doc.objects`, `schema.describe` and `doc.journal`. Nothing in it bypasses a method the protocol has.

**The viewport.** A document becomes a scene through [`doc_scene`](doc_scene.md): each live `engine.scene.MeshPlacement` record is one instance, its `mesh` path (relative to the document) one of the scene's meshes. The renderer draws it into the swapchain image exactly as engine-view draws a scene, at SDR (the overlay composites onto SDR only). After each transaction the editor reads the document's change feed: a placement whose transform changed is moved in place (`SceneRenderer::move_instances`, the next frame draws it there); a placement created, deleted or given another mesh rebuilds the scene (and the overlay's textures are made again). The first camera stands 2.6 bounding radii from the scene's centre (`Viewport::framing_camera`; until stage 2 it stood 0.26 r² metres away, inside any scene smaller than ten metres across). All of it is the renderer's, so engine-host can grow the same viewport over the protocol (`render.load` of a session is the next step and is not built: [doc_scene](doc_scene.md#not-yet)).

**The overlay reaches the screen through the renderer.** ImGui's draw lists are handed to the renderer's overlay pass every frame (`FrameDesc::overlay`, [renderer](renderer.md#the-overlay)), recorded as the frame's last raster pass through `gfx::CommandList`; ImGui's textures (its font atlas) are the overlay's, made and retired as ImGui asks (`ImGuiBackendFlags_RendererHasTextures`). No ImGui backend is compiled, so Vulkan stays in `domain/gfx`, `foundation/window` and the two apps that present, and SDL in `foundation/window`; window events reach ImGui as `window::Event`s (`editor_ui.cpp`), then the editor's pointer and keys (`editor_input.cpp`), and the camera gets what neither took. The layout is built each start (`io.IniFilename` is null: nothing is written beside a run).

**The window is throttled.** vsync, and a ceiling of `editor.present.max_hz` (120; it has no zero) frames a second whatever the present mode, because the editor shares the owner's desktop. `--offscreen` draws the same frames, overlay and gizmo included, into the renderer's own target, which is what `--capture` writes. With the shell, frames are drawn from the page's first `editor.layout`, which places the child window and shows it, and `--frames`, `--pick` and `--drag` count frames from the *last* one, so a scripted pick reads a frame drawn at the size it is aimed at (a layout landing between two frames resizes the picture, and the frame before it is gone — the embedded test found that on its second run); until then the child is hidden, so it never covers a page still loading, and a page that sends no layout within a minute ends the run with exit 1 and the reason. Embedded, the top-level window appears only after the device is made, since the device takes the GPU lock and a window waiting for it would read "Not responding".

**The summary** is one JSON line, the last the run prints, keys sorted: `frames`, `mode`, `width`/`height` (the picture's pixels: the child's, embedded), `records`, `instances`, `selected`, `picked`, `moved`, `rebuilds`, `transactions` and `undo_position` (the session's journal), `pick_ms`, `drags` and `drag` (the last committed drag's `axis`, `delta`, `transaction` and `rationale`, or null), `undos`, `redos`, `deletes`, `embedded` (the web shell was beside the viewport), `listen` (the listener's port, or null), `layouts` (how many `editor.layout`s came) and `viewport` (where the last one put the child, `[x, y, width, height]` in the window's coordinates, or null); `placements` (each time the child moved, the first sixteen: the client size, the rectangle, `clamped`, the page's `narrow`, and `from_page` — false for the editor's own re-placement after a resize or a scale change), `display_scale` and `scale_changes`, `relayouts` and `relayout_reached`, `sdl_keys` (key presses SDL delivered); and what the test flags measured — `look` (the yaw and pitch the gesture turned, in degrees, `relative_asked` — relative mode was asked of the child while the button was held — and `relative`, whether it held the cursor, which only a window in front does), `look_os` (`--test-look-os`: `yaw_deg` and `expected_yaw_deg`, `asked`, `in_effect`, `child_flag` — SDL's own flag on the child, which must stay false — `clip_inside`, `cursor_inside`, `released_by_test`), `keys` (`--test-keys`: whose the keyboard was at each step and what each Ctrl+Z did) — each of the two with `desktop` since A40, what the desktop was doing during the gesture and when Ctrl+Z went to the viewport (`window::desktop_state()`: the editor's own desktop by name, `foreground` — the executable whose window was in front, null when none was, as on a test's private desktop — `foreground_own` and `locked`), so a failure says whose window was in front and whether the session was locked, `page` (`--test-page`: the page's reports), `drops`, `frames_at_drop` and `frames_at_reconnect`; `frames_at_resize`, `frames_at_resize_answer` and `resize_answer_ms` (`--resize-to`: the frame of the resize, the frame the page's layout of the resized window was placed at, and how long after the resize it came, -1 when it did not); and since A39 `gizmo_tool` (the tool at the end), `rotations` and `rotate` (the last committed turn's `axis`, `degrees`, `transaction` and `rationale`, or null), `drops_seen`, `drops_imported` and `drop_error` (files dropped on the window, how many `editor.import_mesh` took, the last refusal). With a listener, the first line is its announcement.

**The test flags** drive the embedded window with nobody at it, counted in frames since the page last moved the viewport as `--pick` is: `--resize-to W,H` resizes the window at frame 4, and **the run ends only once the page has laid out the resized window** (A41, 2026-10-15): the first layout from the page after the window says it was resized that fits the new client area without the clamp is the answer (a layout the page sent for the old size and that arrived late does not fit, and is not), `--frames` then counts from its placement, and a minute after the resize with no answer the editor exits 1 saying so — until then the run ended `--frames` after the editor's own re-placement whether the page had answered or not, and beside two other suites the answer once came after it; `--look DX,DY` holds the right button over the viewport and moves the pointer by (DX, DY) over frames 3 to 7, pushed into SDL's queue as the child's own events — the editor's logic, not the platform's path; `--test-look-os DX,DY` is the platform's path: a move to the child's centre and the right button at frame 3, three moves of (DX, DY) / 3 at frames 4 to 6 and the release at 7, all **posted** to the child (`Window::post_os_event`), and the yaw read at frame 10 against the fly camera's own sensitivity — on a window in the background (a test runner's) the posted moves are the motion the camera reads, so the yaw must be exactly what DX asks — wherever the desktop's cursor is, since A40 (before, SDL read the button from the device on the first move after the press and let go of it unless the cursor happened to be over the child; [window](window.md#child-windows)); in front, the window holds the real cursor, and the run checks the cursor was confined to the child instead (a posted move is not the cursor's own); `--rotate AXIS,DEGREES` (with `--pick`; not a test flag of the embedded window alone, it drives `--no-shell` too) presses R and holds Shift at frame 3, presses on the AXIS ring at frame 4 where it is the ring the pointer hovers, moves round it by DEGREES / 3 at frames 5 to 7 and releases at 8, so the turn snaps to exactly DEGREES; `--test-drop-file <path>` pushes a drop of that file on the viewport through SDL's queue at frame 10; `--test-keys` (with `--move`) posts a click to the child through the **platform's** queue (`Window::post_os_event`, so the window procedure and the platform's focus decide, as for a real click) at frame 10, Ctrl+Z to whoever has the keyboard at 14, gives the page the keyboard (`Webview::focus`) at 18 and posts Ctrl+Z to whoever has it at 22 — never to the desktop's foreground window; `--test-page` loads the page with `&test=1`, registers `editor.test_report` and does not end until the page's checks are in; `--test-relayout-at N` sends `editor.relayout`, and `--test-drop-socket-at N` shuts every connection under the page (`Listener::drop_connections`, the port and token kept), each at frame N once the page's checks are in (and the drop after the relayout's answer), so no report is sent into a socket that is down.

**The fly camera is engine-view's**: `fly_camera.cpp`, `window_input.cpp` and `walk.cpp` from `apps/engine_view` are compiled into the editor rather than copied, so it flies with the same bindings and integration. When a third host wants the camera it should become a module.

**Testing.** `apps/engine_editor/tests/editor_tests.cpp`: with no window and no device, the pointer's maths — a 150 % and an uneven display scale and the ray through a scaled click passing through the point it was aimed at (and missing it by metres unscaled), the nearest point of an axis to a ray and the two cases with none, the handles' length and direction, hover on each handle and on none (the centre's dead zone, between handles, past an end, behind the eye), and a drag's delta far from the world's origin, unchanged when the eye flies with the pointer on the same point, with x and z kept to the bit; the panels' models from a fixture document made by one transaction through the session (outliner order and depth, every descriptor field by kind with defaults, the journal's attribution, undo and redo); offscreen, a pixel of a capture picked back to its record and the same answer from `read_last_frame`, a transaction moving a translation moving the instance in the next frame with no rebuild, a new placement rebuilding; **a gizmo drag through `EditorInput`** — a click picks and times itself, a press on the y handle starts a drag and keeps the selection, the preview moves the instance in the picture while the document and its journal stay as they were, the release commits one transaction of one command named `move left along y` with x and z exact and `sync` following it with a move, Escape restores a second drag's preview and writes nothing, an empty press-and-release writes nothing, Ctrl+Z undoes the drag (a move, not a rebuild), Ctrl+Y redoes it, Z without Ctrl and Delete while something else has the keyboard do nothing, and Delete removes the record in one transaction (a rebuild) that an undo brings back; the overlay composited by the overlay pass (the box and handles differ from the bare picture, the left quarter where a panel once docked is the scene's own, ImGui takes neither pointer nor keys, the ids under it are the scene's); **the three methods through the editor's dispatcher**, with a roles file whose only role may write nothing — `editor.layout` as a notification answered with nothing and recording the rectangle, as a request answering the window's rectangle rounded outward at a ratio of 1.5 and halved where the window's units are two pixels, a negative size refused; `editor.select` changing the selection though `doc.apply` by the same role is refused 1008, a missing record refused with the selection kept, null clearing it; `editor.state` reporting the selection, the session, the layouts, the picture's size and the camera in A32's shape; and a dispatcher with the built-in methods alone answering -32601 for them; the executable in a 320 × 240 vsynced window with `--no-shell` picking the record under its pixel, and **dragging its y handle by a scripted pointer** (`--drag`, in window coordinates, so the case holds under display scaling): the summary names one drag along y in transaction 1, and the document read back afterwards through a session of the test's own has the record moved up y with x and z exactly zero and exactly one transaction after the fixture's; **`--embedded` in a 900 × 560 vsynced window** — the listener announces itself first, the web view loads the shell from it, the page's `editor.layout` places the child window (at least one received, a rectangle of real size, a picture narrower than the window), and a pick at the middle of the child's picture selects the cube, with eight frames drawn after the placement — skipped with the reason where the WebView2 runtime is absent (exit 3); **a change of the display's scale through the dispatcher** (no window): a layout at ratio 1 then the page's answer at 1.5 moves and grows the child by half where the window's coordinates are pixels, the editor's interim placement by the scaled ratio lands on the same rectangle, and where the coordinates are points the rectangle stays and the pick scale goes from 1 to 1.5; the clamp (a cell at the window's edge is not clamped, a cell a CSS pixel past it is cut without being called a clamp, a wider one is clamped and says so); `editor.relayout`'s shape; `editor.test_report` refused until registered; **the narrow layout in a window resized from 1280 × 720 to 800 × 600** (`--resize-to`): the run waited for the page's layout of the resized window, as the drop and reconnect cases wait for theirs, rather than for a count of frames (A41: the answer came at frame `frames_at_resize_answer`, at least eight frames before the end, and the case prints how long after the resize), every layout the page sent placed the child inside the client area without the clamp, the narrow flag was on exactly when the page was under 960 CSS pixels, both sizes were laid out, and the profile is in `<doc>/.editor/webview` beside a `.gitignore` of `*`; **keys and the look gesture** (`--look`, `--test-keys`): the look turned the camera with relative mode asked of the child; **the look through the platform's queue** (`--test-look-os 90,0`): SDL's flag never on the child, and in a window in the background the posted moves turned the camera by exactly 90 pixels of the fly camera's sensitivity (before 2026-10-12 they turned it not at all), in front the cursor held inside the child, a click posted to the child gave it the keyboard, Ctrl+Z posted to it undid `--move`'s transaction (one undo), and with the page given the keyboard a posted Ctrl+Z reached SDL not at all (zero keys, no second undo). **Every window case runs on the test process's own desktop** (A40; `run_editor` through `tests/support/test_desktop.h`: `CreateDesktopW` in the test's window station, named after its process id, the editor started there with `STARTUPINFOW::lpDesktop`, the desktop never shown and closed when the test exits, and the default desktop only where a window station refuses one, which the test says). Until then the two input cases passed or failed by what the owner's desktop was doing — the look whenever another process held the foreground, the keys too under a locked session, both beside other suites' windows — and an editor test window could take the foreground from the person at the desk. On its own desktop the editor's window is on nobody's screen, nothing typed or pointed at reaches it, it is never the platform's foreground window (`GetForegroundWindow` is NULL off the input desktop), the cursor cannot be read there, and every button reads up: one state, the same in every run, a locked session included, and the one in which the window module's message rules ([window](window.md#child-windows)) are what make the posted gesture turn the camera and the posted keys reach the editor — so the look case also requires `in_effect` false there, and both report the desktop's state at the moment; **the page's own checks** (`--test-page`, `--test-relayout-at`, `--test-drop-socket-at`): the command line's completions and the properties' enum list opened by DOM events stay inside their panels and off the cell, `editor.relayout` reached the page and was answered with a layout, and after every connection was shut the pill said "reconnecting", the page came back ("connected", and its "Reconnected" toast off the cell too), and frames went on being drawn between the drop and the reconnect and after it; **the rotate gizmo's maths** (A39): the rings' size, hover on each ring and on none, y winning where it crosses another, a turn far from the world's origin a quarter of the way round the ring reading 90° (and exactly 90 snapped), four quarters reading 360, the same world line from an eye flown three metres giving the same angle to the bit, a ring edge-on refused with the last answer kept, a quarter turn and back and four quarter turns giving back the identity's bits, and with `yaw_deg` authored a turn about x landing where G (Y R) would; **a rotate drag through `EditorInput`**: R and T switch the tool, a press on the y ring (Shift held) starts a turn that keeps the selection, the preview turns the instance while the document stays as it was, the release commits one transaction `rotate left about y by 90°` whose `rotation` is `turned_rotation`'s to the bit with the translation exact, `sync` follows it as a move, Escape restores a second turn and writes nothing, Ctrl+Z undoes it as a move and Ctrl+Y redoes it; **`editor.tool`, `editor.pick_file` and `editor.import_mesh` through the dispatcher**: the tool set and an unknown one refused, the file dialog answering null under test, a `.gltf` with its `.bin` beside it copied under `assets/crate/` with one transaction `import crate.gltf` creating the record on the ground in front of the camera and selecting it, the same bytes imported again reused and not copied, other bytes under the same name refused with the document untouched, a missing file, a text file and a GLB the loader refuses each refused with nothing written, and — when `tools/fetch-samples.ps1` has fetched it (skipped with the reason otherwise) — `content/samples/Suzanne` imported with its buffer and two images, the viewport rebuilding with one more instance whose pixel picks back to the new record, and undo removing it; **the window's rotate gizmo and a drop** (`--rotate y,90`, `--test-drop-file`): one turn about y of 90° committed, read back with the translation exactly as it was, and a GLB dropped on the window imported in a transaction of its own; and `--version` (exit 3 skips the window cases where there is no display). `foundation/webview`'s own test checks the runtime check's reason and that no web view is made without a parent.

**Not yet** (A24): the frame a resize takes the child to follow is hidden by the cell's dark backing, which is there (black) but no test looks at it, since the window test captures nothing; a scale change has been tested through the dispatcher and not by dragging a window between two monitors, which nobody here can script; rotate and scale gizmos and snapping, a multiple selection, the review queue. `Save` is not needed: every committed transaction is already on disk (`Session::apply` saves).

## engine-mcp: the MCP bridge

```
engine-mcp [--host <path>] [--host-arg <arg>]... [--mount <scheme>=<dir>[:rw]]... [--workspace <dir>]
           [--actor <name>] [--role <name>] [--task <id>] [--roles <file.json>]
           [--call-timeout <s>] [--long-call-timeout <s>] [--log <spec>]
engine-mcp --connect ws://127.0.0.1:<port>/ --token <token> [--workspace <dir>] [--actor <name>] ...
engine-mcp --connect-from <file> [--workspace <dir>] [--actor <name>] ...
engine-mcp --help | --version
```

[Plan 06 §6.3](../plan/06-agent-tooling.md#63-mcp-bridge)'s bridge: a Model Context Protocol server on stdin and stdout — newline-delimited JSON-RPC 2.0, MCP's standard stdio transport — that an agent's client (Claude Code, Claude Desktop, any MCP client) starts as a child process. It starts one `engine-host --stdio` of its own and turns curated tools into engine protocol calls. MCP is the cognitive interface and the protocol the mechanical one: the bridge is where the coarse tools, their documentation, attribution rules, pagination, the workspace files and the errors that say what to do next live, and the protocol stays the fine-grained surface the editor, the CLI and the tests use.

**Why a separate process that spawns the host, rather than a second front end on the dispatcher.** The methods an agent needs are not all in one library: `doc.*`, `session.*` and `schema.*` are `domain/protocol`'s, but `render.*` is registered by engine-host over `systems/renderer` ([protocol](protocol.md#methods-an-app-registers-and-render)), and the only thing that has every method is the host. A bridge that linked the dispatcher would have to link the renderer too and would become a second host. As a client it is exactly what engine-cli is — it spawns engine-host through `platform::Process` and writes one request line per call — which is also the plan's shape: the editor, the CLI and this bridge are equal clients of one server ([02](../plan/02-architecture.md)). **One host per bridge** keeps session ownership simple: every session id and scene id an agent sees was made by this bridge's host, nobody else can close them, and they all end together when the client ends the MCP session. Plan 02 describes the bridge as restartable without disturbing the engine; that needs a host that outlives its clients (the persistent-host mode under "Not yet" above), and until one exists the bridge owns its host. Documents do not care: every committed change is on disk before its response, so a new bridge reopens a document exactly where the last one left it.

| Flag | What |
|---|---|
| `--host <path>` | the engine-host to start. Default: beside engine-mcp, as engine-cli finds it. The two are built together: the build makes `engine-mcp` depend on `engine-host`. |
| `--host-arg <arg>` | appended to the host's command line as given; repeatable (`--host-arg --tunables --host-arg t.json`, `--host-arg --log --host-arg info`). Every host the bridge starts gets them, a replacement too. The deadline test passes the host's test hook `--debug-hang` this way. |
| `--mount <scheme>=<dir>[:rw]` | forwarded to the host; repeatable. |
| `--workspace <dir>` | where bulk results go (below). Default `./mcp-workspace` in the directory the client started the bridge in, which `.gitignore` ignores at the repository root. Made absolute at startup and created on the first write. |
| `--actor <name>` | the attribution actor of a mutation that names none. Default `mcp`. |
| `--role <name>`, `--task <id>` | the attribution role and task of a mutation that names none (or names a blank one). No default: without the flag the field is recorded as the call gave it ([below](#attribution-errors-and-the-workspace)). With `--roles`, `--role` is also the role configuration every call runs in ([below](#roles-leases-and-proposals)). The bridge hands `--actor`, `--role` and `--task` to its host as the host's identity. |
| `--roles <file.json>` | role configurations for the host (`content/roles/roles.json`), made absolute and passed on as `engine-host --roles`: the tools the role may not call are not offered, a call cannot name another role, and the host refuses writes outside the role's layers, tiles and types ([below](#roles-leases-and-proposals)). |
| `--call-timeout <s>` | how long a quick call may go unanswered before the host is taken for hung: killed, reported, and replaced on the next call. Seconds, decimals allowed; 0 is no deadline. Default **120** ([Deadlines](#deadlines)). |
| `--long-call-timeout <s>` | the same for the long class — `render.load`, `render.capture`, `render.benchmark`, `render.evaluate`, `render.compare`, `content.build`, `session.run_headless`, `engine.run_tests`, `session.save_game`, `session.load_game`. Default **3600**. |
| `--log <spec>` | log levels on stderr (`info`, `info,mcp=debug`); default warnings and up. |

**stdout belongs to MCP alone.** Every diagnostic — the bridge's log, engine-host's own warnings (its stderr is inherited), a startup failure — goes to stderr, which an MCP client keeps as the server's log. A bridge that cannot start its host exits 2 without writing a byte to stdout; a usage error exits 2; `--help` and `--version` exit 0. **It never opens a socket**, and no named pipe either: it is an anonymous pipe on both sides, so it raises no firewall prompt and there is nothing any other process could connect to (the reader thread of [Deadlines](#deadlines) exists so that it can stay that way), and `tools/ci/check-no-listeners.ps1` (which probes every built executable with `--help`) and `tools/package-tests.ps1` (which takes every `engine-*` binary) cover it with no change.

**MCP, as far as the bridge speaks it.** `initialize` answers with the client's `protocolVersion` when it is one of `2025-11-25`, `2025-06-18`, `2025-03-26` or `2024-11-05`, and with the newest otherwise (the client then decides, which is MCP's rule); the capabilities are `tools` only, with `listChanged: false`, and the result carries `instructions`, a paragraph telling a model how the tools fit together. `notifications/initialized` and every other notification get no answer; `ping` answers `{}`; `tools/list` returns every tool in one page; `tools/call` runs one. What a result may carry follows the agreed version: `title`, tool `annotations` (read-only, destructive and idempotent hints, and `openWorldHint: false` on everything, because a tool touches nothing but this engine and its files), `structuredContent` and `resource_link` blocks each appear from the version that introduced them. Batches are refused with -32600 (MCP dropped them in 2025-06-18), and resources, prompts, completion and logging are -32601: bulk data reaches an agent as files it reads with its own tools, which is [06 §6.1](../plan/06-agent-tooling.md#61-principles)'s rule and needs no resource protocol. A call is answered before the next line is read, so there is never anything in flight for `notifications/cancelled` to cancel.

### The tools

| Tool | Protocol calls | What the bridge adds |
|---|---|---|
| `open_session` | `session.open` | `path` required; a missing document's error says to pass `create: true`. |
| `close_session`, `list_sessions` | `session.close`, `session.list` | |
| `layers`, `add_layer`, `set_edit_layer` | `doc.layers`, `doc.add_layer`, `doc.set_edit_layer` | the stack as one sentence, weakest first, with the edit layer marked. |
| `objects` | `doc.objects` | `limit` (default 50, at most 500) and an opaque `cursor` over the protocol's `offset`; `next_cursor` when there is more; `detail: "summary"` leaves the values out. |
| `get` | `doc.get` | |
| `create_object` | `doc.apply` (one `CreateObject`) | the id is generated (`Id128::generate`) when omitted and returned; `properties` is the command's `value`. |
| `set_property`, `reparent`, `delete_object` | `doc.apply` (one `SetProperty`, `SetParent` or `DeleteObject`) | `reparent` without `parent` moves to the root. |
| `apply` | `doc.apply` | the whole command batch, `atomic` by default. |
| `undo`, `redo` | `doc.undo`, `doc.redo` | "nothing to undo" said as such. |
| `journal` | `doc.journal` | cursor paging; each patch numbered; `detail: "summary"` (the default) gives command counts instead of the commands. |
| `diff` | `doc.diff` | the bridge pages the commands (100 a page by default), since the protocol returns them whole. |
| `merge_layers` | `doc.merge` | the host records its own attribution for a merge. |
| `validate` | `doc.validate` | the diagnostics paged by the bridge. |
| `describe` | `schema.describe`, `engine.methods` | `type` for a schema type, or `method` for a protocol method with its params and result types described. |
| `list_schema` | `schema.types`, `engine.methods` | types filtered by namespace prefix and paged; the method catalogue with the tools that call each method — about 30 KB with its documentation, so `methods` defaults to on only when no `namespace` is given (a narrowed listing asks for it with `methods: true`). |
| `capture` | `render.load`, `render.capture` | `load` or `scene` (below); `out_dir` is the bridge's; the whole result written as `<name>.json`. |
| `benchmark` | `render.load`, `render.benchmark` | the whole result as `<name>.json` and a flythrough's summary as `<name>.jsonl`; a per-pass summary, and whether the machine was quiet. |
| `compare` | `render.compare` | `a` and `b` accept the `file://` URIs other tools returned; `heatmap` (default true) puts the FLIP map in the workspace. Needs no GPU. |
| `evaluate` | `render.load`, `render.evaluate` | as `capture`, into `evaluations/`. |
| `scenes` | `render.scenes` | one line per scene held — source, counts, drawn or not and at what size, texture and ray tracing bytes (and how many images shared a texture another mesh uploaded, with the bytes that saved) — with the process's device memory; each entry the bridge loaded carries the `load` that made it, which is what to pass to get it back. |
| `unload` | `render.unload` | `scene` required; what was released, host heap and device memory before and after in MiB; the bridge forgets the scene, so the same `load` given to a render tool loads it again rather than handing back a dead id. |
| `get_logs` | `log.tail` | `min_level` and a `category` prefix, filtered in the bridge; `next` to continue. |
| `adapters` | `gpu.adapters` | a verdict per device in the answer; the full requirements report as `adapters.json`. |
| `displays` | `gpu.displays` | one line per display output (bits, HDR on or off, minimum, peak and full-frame luminance, SDR white) and what a window there is offered past sRGB; the whole result as `displays.json`. E39's probe; read-only, shows no window. |
| `host_info` | `engine.info`, `engine.ping` | the host's pid, version and uptime with the bridge's workspace, default attribution (actor, and role and task when given), host generation, both deadlines and agreed MCP version. |
| `build_content` | `content.build` | the whole result, every output's metrics included, written as `<workspace>/builds/<name>.json`; the answer is each output's status, path, counts and time, and a failure's rule. |
| `events` | `session.events` | `session` required; a one-line count with the cursor to continue or poll with. |
| `budgets` | `engine.budgets` | one line per budget: used of limit, unit, where the limit comes from. |
| `run_headless` | `session.run_headless` | `session` required; the ticks, the time, why it stopped, how many entities are materialized from the document, and the write-back commits and fields ([protocol](protocol.md#sessionrun_headless-the-materialized-world)). |
| `materialize` | `session.materialize` | `session` required; the counts and one line per record type met: materialized of records, and why any were skipped ([protocol](protocol.md#sessionmaterialize)). |
| `run_tests` | `engine.run_tests` | the counts, and one line per failed check. |
| `state_hash` | `session.state_hash` | `session` required; the hash and each part with its count, and what two runs agreeing on it means ([world](world.md#the-persistent-state-hash)). |
| `save_game` | `session.save_game` | `session` and `path` required; the tick, the files and bytes, the hash, what of a streamed world it kept, and a link to its `save.json` ([protocol](protocol.md#sessionsave_game-sessionload_game-sessionstate_hash)). |
| `load_game` | `session.load_game` | `path` and `dir` required; the new session, the tick, the hash as saved and as loaded with every migration step, and what the next `run_headless` has to hand over to continue the run. |
| `acquire_lease`, `renew_lease`, `release_lease` | `lease.acquire`, `lease.renew`, `lease.release` | the attribution filled from the flags with the rationale optional (a lease records who holds it and for which task); the lease in one line — layer, tiles or types, holder, time left. ([below](#roles-leases-and-proposals)) |
| `leases` | `lease.list` | whether the document requires leases, and one line per live lease with the seconds it has left. |
| `propose_layer` | `doc.propose_layer` | `name`, `target` and a rationale required; the proposal becomes the edit layer by default, so the editing tools write to it. |
| `promote` | `doc.promote` | a promotion in one line with its two journal patches and times; a refused one is an error result listing each conflict and new validation problem, with a hint about `prefer`; a dry run says whether it would promote. |

Of [06 §6.9](../plan/06-agent-tooling.md#69-day-one-operations)'s list, that reaches `open_session`, `diff`, `create_object`, `delete_object`, `set_property`, `reparent`, `describe` (of a schema type or a method; not yet of an object at a detail level), `list_schema`, `capture`, `validate` (the document's schema validation; the content and tissue validators are `run_tests`), `get_logs`, `benchmark`, and — since 2026-09-24 — `build_content`, `events`, `get_budgets` (the `budgets` tool), `run_headless` and `run_tests`. **The bridge's tools are a curated table, not a mirror of the method catalogue**, so those five took a row each in `tools.cpp` (a schema builder over the method's generated params, a runner, and a one-line description) where a new *field* of an existing method takes nothing, and `session.materialize` took a sixth (`materialize`); `list_schema` and `describe` showed the five methods with no change at all. `scenes` and `unload` took two more rows the same way when `render.scenes` and `render.unload` landed, and `host_info` gained `engine.ping` beside `engine.info`; `state_hash`, `save_game` and `load_game` took three when save and load did ([world](world.md#save-and-load)), and the bridge counts the two that copy a world among its long calls. `tunables.*`, `doc.set_partition`, `doc.save` and `session.info` are left to the protocol: each is mechanical, or said better by a tool that is there.

### Input schemas are generated, not written

At startup the bridge asks its host for `engine.methods`, and for each tool takes the params type of the method it calls and walks it with `schema.describe`, turning each field's type as the host spells it (`u32`, `engine.doc.Command[]`, `engine.protocol.RenderCamera?`, `map<string, json>`) into JSON Schema: integers with their ranges, `id128` as a 32-hex-digit pattern, `vec3` as three numbers, `worldpos` as three numbers each in [−137438953408, 137438953408) (the host refuses any other, naming the field; ADR-0053) and `dvec3` as three numbers, `json` as the empty schema, an optional as its element (leaving a field out is how "none" is said), an array as `items`, a string-keyed map as `additionalProperties`, an enum as `enum` with each value's documentation folded into the description (so `CommandKind`'s documentation is the command reference an agent reads), and a struct inline, with `additionalProperties: false` because the protocol refuses a field it does not know. The field documentation is the IDL's `///` comments, and the **defaults are the schema's own**: `schema.describe` reports each field's `default` for exactly this ([protocol](protocol.md)), the value a default-constructed object holds, so `open_session.create` says `false`, `capture.width` says `1280` and `load.settings.raster` says `"hw"` without the bridge knowing any of it. An `f32` default is written as its shortest decimal (24.2277, not 24.227699279785156), and the empty string and the null id are left out, because they are what every string and id without a declared default holds and would read as a suggestion.
