The bridge then says only what the protocol cannot: which fields it fills or hides (`out_dir`, `offset`, `flip_out`, the attribution timestamp the host stamps), which are required (the protocol has no such notion — an absent field is its default), the fields of its own (`load`, `cursor`, `limit`, `detail`, `name`, `heatmap`, `min_level`, `category`, `method`), and prose appended after the engine's. A field added to a params struct therefore reaches the tool by rebuilding the host, with no edit here, and a tool whose method the host does not serve is not offered at all. The top level of every call is checked against the generated schema before anything is sent, so an unknown or missing argument comes back naming the arguments the tool takes. The measured cost of inlining everything: `tools/list` is 69.2 KB for 28 tools, 43.1 KB of it `capture`, `benchmark` and `evaluate`, whose `load` carries the whole of `RenderLoadParams` and `RenderSettings` with their documentation (2026-09-24; it was 66.7 KB that morning, and the 2.5 KB between is `RenderSettings.rt_budget_mib`, which landed that afternoon and reached the five places the settings appear by rebuilding the host and nothing else). With the five day-one operation tools it is 81.9 KB for 33 (measured on `msvc-debug` the same evening, after the cascaded shadow maps' settings had landed too); the five take 8.6 KB of it, 4.4 KB of that `build_content`, whose `options` carry every build flag with its documentation. With `scenes` and `unload` and the attribution defaults' notes it is **83.6 KB for 35** (measured on `msvc-debug`, 2026-09-24, started without `--role` or `--task`): `scenes` is 0.5 KB, `unload` 0.7 KB, and `capture`, `benchmark` and `evaluate` 12.6, 16.7 and 17.9 KB (that count of 35 left out `materialize`, which was there too). With plan 06 §6.5's six tools and the optional `attribution` that `undo`, `redo` and `merge_layers` took from their params types in the same change, it is **96.1 KB for 42** (measured on `msvc-debug`, 2026-09-25, started without `--role` or `--roles`): `acquire_lease` 2.4 KB, `renew_lease` 1.2, `release_lease` 1.0, `leases` 0.7, `propose_layer` 1.8 and `promote` 2.1. By 2026-10-10, when the conformance client first measured it ([below](#the-conformance-client)), it was **146.5 KB for 46 tools** — about 37,000 tokens at four bytes a token, read by an agent's client on every session — because `RenderSettings` had grown to 10.7 KB with its documentation and stood twice in each of `capture`, `benchmark` and `evaluate`.

**Repeated structs are shared through `$defs`, inside each tool.** While a tool's schema is built, every struct's schema carries a marker naming its type; afterwards `share_definitions` (`schema_gen.cpp`) finds each struct type that appears more than once *unchanged* in that one schema, moves its body into the schema's `$defs` under its qualified name, and leaves `{"$ref": "#/$defs/engine.protocol.RenderSettings", "description": …}` where each copy stood — the field's own description, default and deprecation stay beside the reference, since `load.settings` and the per-call `settings` say different things about the same type — then removes the markers. The largest repeated body goes first, so a struct inside a shared one is shared by living in its definition, and the pass repeats until nothing repeats; a copy a tool edited (a field hidden) differs from the rest and is left inline or given a definition of its own. Each `inputSchema` is a document of its own, so nothing is shared *between* tools: a reference into another tool's schema is not something a client resolves. The result is **115.3 KB** (−31.3 KB, 21%), with `capture`, `benchmark` and `evaluate` at 20.1, 18.7 and 19.8 KB, and a struct that comes to be repeated later is shared with no edit here. The form is JSON Schema 2020-12's, MCP's default dialect for `inputSchema` since 2025-11-25; a draft-07 validator resolves the same `$ref` (a JSON pointer works wherever it points) and ignores its siblings, so what an older reader loses is the per-field description of the two settings, not any validation. Claude Code 2.1.293 connected to it, negotiating 2025-11-25 ([below](#registering-it-with-claude-code)).

### Attribution, errors, and the workspace

**Every change through `doc.apply` carries attribution** — `create_object`, `set_property`, `reparent`, `delete_object` and `apply` — because [06 §6.4](../plan/06-agent-tooling.md#64-transactions-checkpoints-rollback) records actor, role, task and rationale with every commit. `attribution` is required; `actor` defaults to `--actor`, and `role` and `task` to `--role` and `--task` when the bridge was started with them — filled in where the call leaves the field out or blank, overridden by any call that gives one, and stated as the field's `default` in the generated schema exactly when there is one (a schema that promised a default the bridge does not fill would be a lie a model acts on). A **missing or blank `rationale` is refused** before anything reaches the host, with a result that says to add one sentence saying why. Without `--roles` the two flags are the attribution half of [06 §6.5](../plan/06-agent-tooling.md#65-permissions-concurrency-and-leases)'s roles and nothing more. **With `--roles`, `--role` is the name that selects a role configuration** ([below](#roles-leases-and-proposals)): its allowed tools, layers, tiles, types and review rule come with it, and the attribution's `role` stays the name it was selected by, so a journal written before role configurations existed reads the same. `--task` is recorded with every lease the agent takes (06 §6.8's ledger task, when the ledger exists). A transaction the host rejects (`committed: false`) is an error result listing every diagnostic, and says that an atomic batch rolled back whole. `doc.merge`, `doc.undo`, `doc.redo` and `doc.add_layer` take no attribution in the protocol, and `merge_layers` records the host's own.

**Errors come in two kinds.** A message that is not a request the bridge can serve — unparseable (-32700, id null), a batch or not an object (-32600), an unknown method (-32601), an unknown tool or `arguments` that are not an object (-32602, with the tool names in `data`) — is a JSON-RPC error, and the bridge keeps serving. Everything that goes wrong *inside* a tool is a tool result with `isError: true`, which a model reads and can act on: the first line says what failed, then the protocol's code and name (`error 1003 NotFound: no document at …`), then each `{path, message}` diagnostic of an invalid-params error, then `Hint:` and one sentence of what to do, chosen by code and, where it helps, by method (`session.open`'s NotFound says to pass `create: true`; a missing scene says to pass `load`; SessionNotFound says sessions do not survive a host restart). `structuredContent.error` carries the same fields. **Error 1007 reads "No GPU on this machine can do this: …"** with the adapter's own verdict sentence and a hint that says the document tools still work, so an agent on a CI runner or a Pascal box is told the machine's limit rather than a mistake in its request.

**A host that dies is noticed on the next call**, which finds its stdin refusing the write or its stdout ending before the answer: a dead process closes its pipes at once. The bridge reaps it and answers `engine-host exited with code N; …` as an error result whose hint says the next call starts a new host and that open sessions and loaded scenes did not survive (documents are on disk: `open_session` again). The next call does start one, and `host_info` reports its generation. On POSIX the bridge ignores SIGPIPE, so a write to a dead host is a failed write rather than the signal that would end the bridge too. A host that is alive and never answers is the deadline's ([below](#deadlines)).

**The workspace.** Tools that produce files write them under `--workspace` and return `file://` URIs — in the result's text, in `structuredContent`, and as `resource_link` content blocks — with a short summary. Nothing bulk is ever inlined: a capture's answer is under 8 KB whatever the resolution, and the end-to-end suite asserts it.

```
<workspace>/captures/<name>.png, <name>.ids.bin, <name>.ids.json, <name>.depth.png, <name>.normals.png, <name>.json
<workspace>/benchmarks/<name>.json   (and <name>.jsonl: the flythrough summary line engine-view's --benchmark file ends with)
<workspace>/compares/<name>.flip.png, <name>.json
<workspace>/evaluations/<name>.realtime.png, <name>.reference.png, <name>.flip.png, ..., <name>.json
<workspace>/adapters.json
<workspace>/displays.json
```

`<name>.json` is always the host's whole result, so the summary can stay short without losing anything. A `name` the caller gives is used as given, so a `baseline` can be rewritten on purpose; a generated one is `capture-001`, `capture-002`, … — the first number whose `<name>.json` is free, so two calls, or two bridge runs on one workspace, never overwrite each other.

**Scenes.** A render tool takes `load` — `RenderLoadParams`, generated like every other schema: a mesh, a scene file, or a procedural scene, with `settings` — or the `scene` id an earlier render tool returned. The host keeps every scene it loads until `unload` releases it or the host exits, so the bridge remembers each distinct `load` (by its canonical JSON) per host and reuses the scene when the same `load` comes again; the result says `"reused": true`. **That reuse is why a long session needs `unload`**: an agent comparing twenty assets loads twenty scenes, and each one's GPU state — buffers, textures, acceleration structures, a renderer sized to the last frame it drew — stays until the host goes, which behind engine-mcp is when the agent's client goes. `scenes` shows what is held (and, for each, the `load` that made it), `unload` lets one go, and the bridge forgets its `load` at the same moment, so the next render tool given that `load` loads it again instead of naming a dead id. A scene is the host's and not a session's: closing a document unloads nothing ([protocol](protocol.md#methods-an-app-registers-and-render) says why). A new host, after a death or a deadline, starts with none.

### Roles, leases and proposals

[Plan 06 §6.5](../plan/06-agent-tooling.md#65-permissions-concurrency-and-leases) through the bridge; the mechanism is the host's ([protocol](protocol.md#roles-leases-and-proposals)), and the bridge adds three things.

**The role configuration decides which tools are offered.** At startup the bridge asks its host for `engine.roles`; when a roles file is loaded, the role its calls run in — `--role`, or the file's `default_role` for a bridge started without one — lists the methods it may call (every read-only method, and the writes its patterns name), and a tool is offered only when it may call all of that tool's methods. A QA bridge is not shown `create_object` at all rather than shown it and refused: a tool that always fails only teaches a model to call it. A model that calls one anyway, by a name it remembers from another session or this page, gets the JSON-RPC error MCP gives an unknown tool (-32602) with the reason in it — `tool 'create_object' is not offered to role 'qa': it calls doc.apply, which that role may not call. The role is chosen when engine-mcp is started (--role), not by a call` — and a tool missing because the host does not serve its method says that instead; "unknown tool" is kept for names the table does not have (the conformance client found the bare "unknown tool" on its first run). The host checks every call anyway. `host_info` reports the configuration and how many tools were withheld.

**A call cannot name another role.** Under a role configuration, an attribution whose `role` is not the bridge's is refused before it reaches the host, with a hint that the role is chosen when the bridge is started. The protocol has no authentication — a role is what a call says it is — so this is the one place an agent is kept in the role it was given; the bridge is also what hands the host `--actor`, `--role` and `--task`, so a call that names no role (`add_layer`, `undo`) runs in the bridge's.

**Leases and proposals are six tools**: `acquire_lease`, `renew_lease`, `release_lease`, `leases`, `propose_layer`, `promote` (the table above). The protocol's `lease.require`, `doc.reject` and `engine.roles` are left to it: a director's switches, and what the bridge reads for itself. The working protocol an agent follows is **acquire, read, edit, promote, release** — in particular a lease is released after the proposal is promoted, not after the edit, because an edit unmerged in a proposal is exactly what the lease protects ([E12](../experiments/e12-proposal-layers-and-leases.md) found what releasing early costs). Errors 1008 Forbidden, 1009 LeaseConflict and 1010 LeaseRequired come back with hints that say which of those steps is missing.

### Deadlines

Every protocol call the bridge makes waits for its answer with a **deadline**, and a host that stays silent past it is taken for hung: the bridge kills it, answers the tool call with an error that names the method and the seconds waited (`engine-host did not answer session.list within 120 s, so the bridge stopped it`), and starts a new host on the next call — generation + 1, exactly the shape a host that died already had, and with the same hint (sessions and scenes did not survive; documents are on disk), plus which flag to raise if the call was legitimately long. `structuredContent.error` carries `timed_out: true` and `seconds`. Before this a host that was alive and never answered — a driver stuck in a call, a deadlock — blocked the bridge, and with it the agent, forever.

**Two classes, two defaults.** A call's deadline is the one of its class:

| Class | Methods | Default | Flag |
|---|---|---|---|
| long | `render.load`, `render.capture`, `render.benchmark`, `render.evaluate`, `render.compare`, `content.build`, `session.run_headless`, `engine.run_tests`, `session.save_game`, `session.load_game` | **3600 s** | `--long-call-timeout` |
| quick | everything else: `doc.*`, `session.*` but `run_headless`, `save_game` and `load_game`, `schema.*`, `engine.*` but `run_tests`, `log.tail`, `gpu.adapters`, `gpu.displays`, `render.unload`, `render.scenes`, and the startup's own `engine.methods` and `schema.describe` | **120 s** | `--call-timeout` |

The long class is the work whose time grows with the content or with what was asked for; the quick class is bookkeeping over a document or the host's own state, where a host that takes two minutes has stopped. 0 turns a class's deadline off. **A reference render does not come near the hour.** The corpus's heaviest reference (`content/test-scenes/`, 1024 samples at 640×480, three bounces) took 0.28 s of tracing and 3.4 s for the whole `render.evaluate` on the development machine's RTX 5090, and a 4,096-sample reference at 1920×1080 took 2.4 s and 17.5 s — both in `msvc-debug`, whose image metrics are most of that wall time, beside other work (others 21% of the CPU, the GPU 13% busy: upper bounds). That is over two hundred times inside the long deadline for a render sixteen times the corpus's largest, and the baseline-tier GPUs cannot run the reference at all. What *can* pass an hour is a request far outside anything the project runs — `reference.spp` goes to 65,536 and a frame to 16384×16384 — and that caller raises `--long-call-timeout` or sets it to 0; the error's hint says so.

**How the read waits: a reader thread.** The read of the host's stdout needs a timeout, and a blocking pipe read has none. POSIX would give one through `poll` on the descriptor, but Windows gives one only through overlapped I/O, which anonymous pipes (`CreatePipe`, what `platform::Process` makes) do not support; the only pipe that does is a *named* one, an object in the machine's pipe namespace that another local process could open between its creation and the child's — a listener in all but name, which this bridge must not have. So each host gets one reader thread (`host_client.cpp`), started with it and joined when it is reaped: the thread blocks in the kernel's read — no busy loop, no sleep between polls — hands each whole line over under a mutex, and wakes the caller through a condition variable whose `wait_until` is the deadline. One code path on both platforms, and the pipes stay anonymous. On a deadline the host is killed, which closes its end of the pipe and ends the thread's read; on Windows the read is also cancelled (`CancelSynchronousIo`), because a process the host started inherits that end — `CreateProcess` passes every inheritable handle, and the machine-state sampler runs nvidia-smi around every benchmark — and would otherwise hold the join until it exited. The write of a request has no deadline of its own: a host that answered the previous call is back in its read and takes a whole line before dispatching it, so a hang is always after the write, where the read's deadline is. The bridge's own shutdown is bounded the same way — a host still there the short deadline after its input closed is killed rather than waited for.

**The test hook.** A hung host is made on purpose with engine-host's `--debug-hang <method>` (under **engine-host** at the top of this page), handed to it through `--host-arg`, so the end-to-end case needs no fault injection in the bridge: `--call-timeout 1 --host-arg --debug-hang --host-arg session.list` makes `list_sessions` come back after one second (measured 1.004 s) with the error, a new pid and generation 2 on the next call; `--long-call-timeout 3` with `content.build` hung makes `build_content` come back after three (3.013 s) and not after the quick deadline of one.

### The conformance client

```powershell
pwsh tools/mcp-check.ps1 [-Mcp build/msvc-debug/bin/engine-mcp.exe] [-Scratch <dir>] [-Report <file.json>]
                         [-ProtocolVersion 2025-11-25] [-NoRender] [-KeepScratch]
```

`tools/mcp-check.ps1` drives engine-mcp from start to finish as an agent's client does, and nothing in it knows the bridge's C++: it starts `engine-mcp --workspace <scratch>/workspace --actor mcp-check`, speaks newline-delimited JSON-RPC over the pipes (`initialize` with a protocol version, `notifications/initialized`, `tools/list`, then `tools/call` with increasing ids), and runs [06 §6.10](../plan/06-agent-tooling.md#610-multi-agent-roles-review-and-the-human-director)'s one-agent loop as a script of tool calls: open (create) a session, find a type with `list_schema` and read it with `describe`, create an object and set a property with a rationale each, read the journal, add a layer and read the diff, validate, capture a frame, benchmark a short camera path the check writes itself, read the log tail, undo, close. Around the loop it asserts the plan's invariants on the wire: every edit is **one** journal patch of one command carrying the actor and its rationale; an edit with no attribution or a blank rationale is refused with a hint and journals nothing; a notification gets no answer (a `ping` sent straight after must be the next line); every tool has a description, an object schema whose `required` names its own properties, and a `readOnlyHint`; a capture's answer is under 8 KB and every `resource_link` names a file that exists; a closed session's id answers with a hint. Then two more bridges on the same document under `content/roles/roles.json`: under **`qa`** the read-only tools are offered and answer (`open_session`, `objects`, `journal`, `validate`, `layers`, `describe`), no read-only tool is withheld, the writing tools are not offered and a call to one is refused; under **`designer`** a direct `set_property` comes back as 1008 with the hint pointing at `propose_layer`. The render steps skip with the reason — never fail — where the bridge answers 1007, so the check runs on a CI runner as it does here. It records, per tool, the response time and the byte size of its `inputSchema` (measured on the raw `tools/list` text, as the bridge wrote it) and of its result, prints a table and the size of the whole list, writes every line both ways to `<scratch>/transcript.jsonl` and each bridge's stderr beside it, and with `-Report` writes the steps and the measurements as JSON. Exit 0 when every step passed or skipped, 1 when one failed, 2 when there was no engine-mcp to start. It is a test, so it owns nothing outside its scratch directory: `-Scratch` names one, and without it a fresh GUID directory under the system temp directory is made and removed (kept when a step failed).

It runs under CTest as **`mcp_bridge.mcp_check`** (`tools/mcp-check.Tests.ps1`, with the built `engine-mcp` passed as `-Mcp`, so `tools/dev.ps1 test -Affected` runs it whenever the bridge changes), which also proves the check is not vacuous: a stand-in server written by the test, which answers a notification and accepts an edit with no rationale, must fail the check with both named and the table still printed, and a missing binary must exit 2.

**What the first run found (2026-10-10, `msvc-debug`, RTX 5090, beside other agents' builds, so the times are upper bounds).** The loop ran end to end on the first attempt once the client's own bugs were out, and every invariant above held. What it showed, and what became of each:

| Finding | What the client showed | Now |
|---|---|---|
| `tools/list` was **146.5 KB** for 46 tools (~37k tokens at four bytes a token), not the 96.1 KB this page last measured: `RenderSettings` had grown to 10.7 KB with its documentation and stood twice in each of `capture` (30.6 KB), `benchmark` (29.2) and `evaluate` (30.3) | the per-tool schema sizes | `$defs` inside each render tool's schema ([above](#input-schemas-are-generated-not-written)): **115.3 KB** (~29k tokens), the three at 20.1, 18.7 and 19.8 KB; under `qa`, 112.2 → 81.0 KB for 29 tools |
| A tool withheld by the role, called by name, answered `unknown tool 'create_object'`, which reads as a typo or a broken server | the `qa` step's refusal text | `tool 'create_object' is not offered to role 'qa': it calls doc.apply, which that role may not call. The role is chosen when engine-mcp is started (--role), not by a call` ([roles](#roles-leases-and-proposals)) |
| `list_schema` with a `namespace` still sent the whole method catalogue: **30.9 KB** to learn that `engine.content` holds two types | `list_schema`'s result size | the catalogue defaults to on only when no namespace is given: the same call is 368 bytes |
| The journal said "Journal patches none of 0; the first 0 are applied and the rest can be redone" of an empty journal, and "the rest can be redone" when nothing could be | the summary text | "The journal is empty: nothing has been committed."; "…, all applied."; "the last 1 were undone and can be redone." |
| The page and the test said 45 tools; there are 46 (`materialize` was offered and never listed) | the tool count | the test names all 46 and checks the count |
| A capture made without a camera said nowhere which camera the host framed it with — not in the answer, not in `<name>.json` — so an agent could not capture the same view again, benchmark a path from it, or say what it was looking at, and 06 §6.6 makes the camera part of a capture's provenance | building the benchmark's path from the capture | fixed 2026-10-10 in the host: every `render.capture` result carries `camera`, and so `<name>.json` does (roadmap [A32](../roadmap.md); [engine-host --listen](#engine-host---listen-the-protocol-over-a-loopback-websocket)); the conformance client does not yet build its path from it |

Per tool, from the run after the fixes (the input schema before and after where `$defs` changed it):

| Tool | Calls | Slowest ms | Schema bytes before | after | Largest result bytes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `initialize` (the host starting included) | 1 | 470–2390 | | | 1,269 |
| `tools/list` | 1 | 74 | | | 146,533 → 115,280 |
| `open_session` | 3 | 42 | 513 | 513 | 1,356 |
| `list_schema` | 1 | 21 | 623 | 693 | 30,903 → 368 |
| `describe` | 2 | 8 | 319 | 319 | 4,964 |
| `create_object` | 4 | 23 | 1,413 | 1,413 | 830 |
| `set_property` | 3 | 8 | 1,145 | 1,145 | 901 |
| `journal` | 7 | 5 | 667 | 667 | 1,665 |
| `add_layer` | 1 | 5 | 1,585 | 1,585 | 698 |
| `diff` | 1 | 3 | 636 | 636 | 764 |
| `validate` | 2 | 3 | 459 | 459 | 241 |
| `capture` (320×180, color and ids, the scene loaded) | 1 | 742 | 30,575 | 20,134 | 4,963 |
| `benchmark` (a 15-frame path, the scene reused) | 1 | 831 | 29,180 | 18,739 | 6,883 |
| `get_logs` | 1 | 13 | 601 | 601 | 15,880 |
| `undo` | 1 | 6 | 697 | 697 | 231 |
| `get`, `layers`, `objects`, `list_sessions`, `close_session`, `host_info` | 1–2 | ≤ 3 | 62–909 | same | ≤ 2,000 |

Everything but the two render calls answers in milliseconds; what an agent pays for is the list it reads once per session, and after `$defs` the three render tools are still half of it (`RenderSettings`' documentation is most of that, and is what makes the settings usable without reading the engine's source).

### Registering it with Claude Code

Build a preset, then register the binary. For yourself, in this project (`--scope local`, kept in your own Claude Code settings rather than in the tree, because the command line names paths on your machine):

```powershell
tools/dev.ps1 build -Preset msvc-release
claude mcp add --transport stdio --scope local engine -- `
  D:/workspace/game_engine/build/msvc-release/bin/engine-mcp.exe `
  --workspace D:/workspace/game_engine_local/mcp-workspace --actor claude
```

or the same as an `.mcp.json` entry (`--scope project` writes one at the repository root; do not commit one with machine paths in it):

```json
{
  "mcpServers": {
    "engine": {
      "type": "stdio",
      "command": "D:/workspace/game_engine/build/msvc-release/bin/engine-mcp.exe",
      "args": ["--workspace", "D:/workspace/game_engine_local/mcp-workspace", "--actor", "claude"]
    }
  }
}
```

**What registering took, the first time (2026-10-10).** On the development machine the `claude` CLI is not on `PATH`: it is the desktop app's own copy (`%APPDATA%\Claude\claude-code\<version>\<hash>\claude.exe`, 2.1.293 that day). Started with the entry above in a file of its own — `claude -p "…" --mcp-config <file> --strict-mcp-config`, which writes no setting — it spawned engine-mcp, connected over stdio in 861 ms, negotiated **2025-11-25** and saw the tools capability, which is the whole of the server's side of registration; the session then stopped at "Not logged in", because that copy signs in through the desktop app and has no login of its own, and signing in is the owner's to do. So no tool was called from a Claude Code session by an agent; [the conformance client](#the-conformance-client) is the evidence that the tools work as a client sees them. A project-scope `.mcp.json` shows in `claude mcp list` as "Pending approval (run `claude` to approve)", an interactive approval given once. Two things the attempt taught: Claude Code probes a stdio server's protocol era by spawning it, and a server that exits before `initialize` (a mistyped argument did) is respawned on the legacy path with its stderr — engine-mcp's usage text — in Claude Code's MCP log, `%LOCALAPPDATA%\claude-cli-nodejs\Cache\<project>\mcp-logs-engine\*.jsonl`, which is where to look when a registration does not come up. **What an agent sees first** is `initialize`'s 1,040 characters of instructions and the `tools/list`: 115.3 KB, roughly 29,000 tokens (146.5 KB and 37,000 before `$defs`), or 81.0 KB for the 29 tools a `qa` bridge offers.

`/mcp` in Claude Code then lists the server and its 46 tools. Add `--role` and `--task` to the arguments to have every edit's attribution carry them, `--roles D:/workspace/game_engine/content/roles/roles.json` to make `--role` a role configuration that restricts what the agent may change, and `--long-call-timeout` if the session will ask for renders past the hour. Two things are worth knowing first. **A running server locks its executables on Windows**: while a Claude Code session has engine-mcp open, `engine-mcp.exe` and the `engine-host.exe` it started cannot be replaced, so rebuilding that preset fails at the link until the session ends or `/mcp` reconnects — register a preset you are not rebuilding, or a copy of its `bin/` directory. And a render tool on this shared machine is GPU work like any other: `benchmark` numbers carry the machine state and say when they are upper bounds ([bench](bench.md#measuring-on-a-shared-machine)).

### Testing

`tools/dev.ps1 test -Filter mcp_bridge` runs `apps/mcp_bridge/tests/mcp_tests.cpp`, which drives engine-mcp as a client does, one line in and one line out, with a `TempDir` workspace and documents: `--help`, `--version`, an unknown flag (exit 2) and a host that cannot start (exit 2, nothing on stdout); the version handshake (the version asked for echoed, an older one echoed, an unknown one answered with the newest), the tools capability and the instructions, and a notification that gets no answer; `tools/list` — every tool with a name, a description and an object schema whose `required` names its own properties, all 46 checked by name (`displays` and `materialize` among them) and the count checked against the list, so a tool added without being named fails, and the evidence that the schemas are generated (a boolean `create` defaulting to `false` with the IDL's documentation, `capture.width` defaulting to 1280, `RenderSettings.raster` to `"hw"` in the `$defs` of `capture`, which `load.settings` and the per-call `settings` both reference with their own descriptions — as `benchmark`'s and `evaluate`'s do — and no type marker left in any of the three, an `f32` default as 24.2277, the `CommandKind` enum with its value documentation, `out_dir` and `offset` hidden, the attribution requiring `rationale` with `--actor` as the actor's default and no default for `role` or `task` when the flags are absent, `unload` requiring the `scene` of `render.unload`'s own params and `scenes` taking none, `scenes` read-only and `unload` destructive); **`--role` and `--task`** — both stated as defaults in the generated attribution schema, filled into a mutation that gives only a rationale, overridden by one that gives its own role, and filled again where the call's task is blank, as the journal shows, and reported by `host_info`; **`--roles`**, over three bridges one after another on one document — a QA bridge offered `objects`, `leases` and `capture` but not `create_object`, `propose_layer` or `acquire_lease`, with `host_info` naming the role and the tools withheld, a call to `create_object` refused with -32602 naming the role, `doc.apply` and `--role`, and still able to open (create) the document; a designer (review required) refused a direct edit with the 1008 hint pointing at `propose_layer`, then proposing, creating in its proposal, refused by the bridge for naming the director's role, refused promotion with "needs review", and acquiring, listing and releasing a lease that records its actor and role; and a director's bridge promoting the designer's proposal off the disk; a scripted session — open with create, three objects (one id generated), two pages of objects and a summary page, set, get, undo, get, redo, `list_schema` narrowed to `engine.content` without the method catalogue and with it when asked, and the whole listing with it, the journal with the actor from `--actor`, a layer with an override and the diff, reparent, delete, validate, a batch through `apply`, list and close, then SessionNotFound with its hint and a missing document's `create: true` hint; refusals — no attribution, a blank rationale, no rationale, nothing journaled and the empty journal saying so in words ("The journal is empty"), and a transaction the host rejects reported as nothing committed; JSON-RPC errors for an unknown tool, a malformed line, a batch, an unknown method and non-object arguments, tool errors for a misspelt and a missing argument and an invalid-params protocol error with its diagnostic, and the bridge still answering after all of them; **the no-GPU answer on every machine**, by starting the bridge with the Vulkan loader's drivers hidden (`VK_DRIVER_FILES`, `VK_ICD_FILENAMES` and `VK_LOADER_DRIVERS_DISABLE`, inherited by the host), which makes `capture` answer 1007 as "No GPU on this machine" and `adapters` say the same while `open_session` still works — a loader that ignores the override (an elevated one does) renders instead, and the case says which happened; the render round trip where there is a GPU — a 64×48 capture of the heightfield with color and ids, the files where the URIs say and the id file its exact size, the answer under 8 KB with the color as a `resource_link`, the same `load` reused as the same scene under the next generated name, a capture by scene id, `compare` over the two URIs with its heat map, a four-frame benchmark with its result file, then `scenes` listing the one scene with the `load` that made it, `unload` releasing it (GPU state and all, none left), a capture of its id and a second unload both refused as unloaded, `scenes` empty, and the same `load` loading it again under a new id rather than reusing the dead one — recorded as `RENDERED on <adapter>`, or `SKIPPED` with the no-GPU text; **with nothing loaded, on every machine**, `scenes` answers an empty list and `unload` of an id never handed out is NotFound with a hint pointing at `scenes`; a host killed by pid (from `host_info`), reported as `engine-host exited` on the next call and replaced by a new host (another pid, generation 2) on the one after; and **the deadlines** ([above](#deadlines)): a host hung on `session.list` by `--debug-hang` answered after the one-second quick deadline with the method and the seconds in the error, `timed_out` in the data and the quick flag in the hint, then a new host (another pid, generation 2, an uptime) that opens a document; a host hung on `content.build` answered after the three-second long deadline and not the one-second quick one; and `--call-timeout` of `soon`, `-1` and `1e9` each refused with exit 2 before any host starts. The render case is the only one that touches the GPU, and its name has no comma so that doctest's filter can name it: `engine_mcp_bridge_tests -tce="*renders into*"` runs the rest. The same filter also selects **`mcp_bridge.mcp_check`**, the [conformance client](#the-conformance-client) run against the built engine-mcp, which drives the bridge from outside rather than through this suite's helpers and takes about ten seconds in `msvc-debug` (9.8 s beside the suite's 14.8 s, on the development machine with other agents building).

### Attached to a running host: --connect

```powershell
engine-editor --doc D:\worlds\desert                      # or: engine-host --listen 127.0.0.1:0 --doc <dir>
# its first line on stdout, saved to a file: {"listen":"ws://127.0.0.1:52341/","token":"...","session":"s1",...}
engine-mcp --connect-from host.json --actor claude          # or: --connect ws://127.0.0.1:52341/ --token <token>
```

(2026-10-11, roadmap A6.) The bridge becomes a **client of a host somebody else runs** — engine-editor, or engine-host `--listen` — over its loopback WebSocket with its token (foundation/net's client half), instead of starting its own: the same tools, and `open_session` of the host's document path joins the host's session (a session manager returns the session already open on a path), so **an agent at Claude Code edits the document the owner has open in engine-editor, and the owner sees each edit arrive** through the shell's subscription. `--connect-from` reads the host's first stdout line from a file — `listen`, `token`, and the `session`, which the bridge names in its instructions to the model. The URL must be `ws://127.0.0.1:<port>/` (or `localhost`): loopback only, as the listener binds.

- **The host is not the bridge's.** It is not started, not stopped, not killed: a call that outlives its deadline is reported as before but the connection is kept and the host left running (its late answer is recognised by id and dropped); a closed connection is reported and the next call connects again; the bridge's exit closes its connection and nothing else.
- **The host's flags are refused**: `--host`, `--host-arg`, `--mount` and `--roles` configure a host the bridge starts, so with `--connect` each exits 2 naming it. The bridge's `--actor`, `--role` and `--task` still travel in each mutation's attribution as they do spawned, and the host's own roles file still governs what that role may change; when the host has one, `engine.roles` still decides which tools are offered. `--workspace` stays the bridge's: bulk results are the bridge's files, written by the host to the absolute paths the bridge names — the brief asked that it be refused, but it is not the host's in either mode, and refusing it would only fix it at `./mcp-workspace`. Against engine-editor, whose dispatcher has no `render.*`, the render tools are not offered, as on any host without the method.
- **Events become MCP notifications.** On every connection the bridge subscribes to the host's `journal` and `scene` events for every session, and passes each on as **`notifications/resources/updated`** with the resource's URI — `engine://session/<id>/journal` or `engine://scene/<id>` — to an MCP client that subscribed to that resource (`resources/subscribe`). That rather than `notifications/message` because it is MCP's own word for "this thing changed", it reaches only a client that asked, it carries the URI and nothing else (as the host's events carry ids and nothing else), and every protocol version the bridge declares, 2024-11-05 on, has it. The resources themselves exist in both modes (`resources/list`: each open session's journal and each loaded scene; `resources/read`: the journal's newest twenty patches, or the scene's `render.scenes` entry, read through the host under the bridge's role); `initialize` declares `resources.subscribe` true only when attached, since a spawned host on stdio cannot push. The notifications are written from the socket's reader thread under the same lock as the main loop's answers, so a line on stdout is always one whole message.
- **Progress notifications do not come with it.** The host pushes events about committed state, not progress of a call in flight; a long `render.benchmark` or `content.build` still has no channel for progress (below).

**Testing.** `mcp_tests.cpp` ("--connect takes a loopback ws:// URL...") holds the refusals: each spawned host's flag, a non-loopback URL, no token, a token alone, a `--connect-from` file that is not an announcement, and nobody listening (exit 2, nothing on stdout). `tools/mcp-check.ps1 -Connect` (CTest `mcp_bridge.mcp_check_connect`) runs the conformance loop attached to an `engine-host --listen` it starts, beside a second, plain WebSocket client (.NET's) subscribed to the session's journal: the bridge's `open_session` joins the host's session; the second client is pushed an `event.journal` for each of the bridge's commits and reads them, with the bridge's actor and rationales, in `doc.journal` over its own connection — the agent and the owner on one session of one host; the MCP client, subscribed to the journal resource, is sent `notifications/resources/updated` for it; the roles steps skip, since `--roles` is refused attached. Measured on this shared machine (other agents building, another agent's frame budgets holding the GPU, so upper bounds): an edit's `tools/call` to the second client's event **15.3 ms** in msvc-debug and **10.1 ms** in msvc-release, and the `doc.objects` the shell's outliner sends on that event 8.5 ms and 3.0 ms more — the shell's outliner follows an agent's edit in about 25 ms plus the page's own layout, where the poll it replaced took up to two seconds.

### Not yet

- **Prompts.**
- **Progress notifications** for a long `evaluate` or reference `capture`. The host has no channel for them: its dispatcher answers a request with one line and writes nothing else, and the one place progress exists — the reference renderer's batch loop — has no way out of `render_methods.cpp`. It would take a per-batch callback on `renderer::ReferenceRenderer::render`, the host writing JSON-RPC notifications (no `id`) on stdout while the call runs, and the bridge forwarding each as MCP `notifications/progress` when the `tools/call` carried `_meta.progressToken`. The bridge half is small now: the reader thread of [Deadlines](#deadlines) already sees every line the host writes while a call waits, and skips what is not the answer with a warning; a notification would be forwarded instead. `session.events` is not that channel — it is a cursor a client polls over a document's journal, and a render has no document.
- **A deadline for one call.** Both deadlines are the bridge's flags; a tool argument that raised them for one render would let an agent ask for a render past the hour without restarting its server.
- **Tools for a director's switches** — `lease.require`, `doc.reject` — and for reading `engine.roles` directly. They are protocol methods an agent can reach through engine-cli; a tool is added when an agent's loop needs one ([06 §6.3](../plan/06-agent-tooling.md#63-mcp-bridge)'s rule).
- **A benchmark path from a capture's camera.** Every capture now says which camera it was framed with (roadmap [A32](../roadmap.md), `camera` in the result and in `<name>.json`), but the conformance client still builds its benchmark path without reading it.
- **A smaller `tools/list`.** After `$defs` it is 115.3 KB, half of it the three render tools and most of that `RenderSettings`' documentation. The next step would be a detail level for the settings (the knobs an agent reaches for spelled out, the rest one object it reads `describe` for), which trades schema-checked arguments for size; not taken while no client limits a server's list.
